            for (int j = 0; j < half; j++) {
                double wr = Math.cos(ang * j), wi = Math.sin(ang * j);       // root of unity
                double xr = re[i + j + half] * wr - im[i + j + half] * wi;   // v = a[i + j + half] * w
                double xi = re[i + j + half] * wi + im[i + j + half] * wr;
                re[i + j + half] = re[i + j] - xr;                          // a[i+j] - v
                im[i + j + half] = im[i + j] - xi;
                re[i + j] += xr;                                            // a[i+j] + v
                im[i + j] += xi;
            }
    }
    if (invert) for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { re[i] /= n; im[i] /= n; }
}

// product of two integer polynomials (coefficients below about 1e15 in the result)
static long[] multiply(long[] a, long[] b) {
    int need = a.length + b.length - 1, n = 1;
    while (n < need) n <<= 1;                           // power of two at least la + lb - 1
    double[] ar = new double[n], ai = new double[n], br = new double[n], bi = new double[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) ar[i] = a[i];
    for (int i = 0; i < b.length; i++) br[i] = b[i];
    fft(ar, ai, false);
    fft(br, bi, false);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {                       // pointwise complex product
        double r = ar[i] * br[i] - ai[i] * bi[i];
        ai[i] = ar[i] * bi[i] + ai[i] * br[i];
        ar[i] = r;
    }
    fft(ar, ai, true);
    long[] c = new long[need];
    for (int i = 0; i < need; i++) c[i] = Math.round(ar[i]);   // exact integers after rounding
    return c;
}
--py
import cmath

def fft(a, invert):
    """In-place FFT of a list of complex numbers; the length must be a power of two."""
    n = len(a)
    lg = floor_log2(n)
    for i in range(n):                                  # bit-reversal permutation
        r = 0
        for b in range(lg):
            if get_bit(i, b):
                r = set_bit(r, lg - 1 - b)
        if i < r:
            a[i], a[r] = a[r], a[i]
    length = 2
    while length <= n:
        half = length // 2
        ang = 2 * cmath.pi / length * (-1 if invert else 1)
        for i in range(0, n, length):
            for j in range(half):
                w = cmath.exp(1j * ang * j)             # root of unity
                u, v = a[i + j], a[i + j + half] * w
                a[i + j] = u + v
                a[i + j + half] = u - v
        length <<= 1
    if invert:
        for i in range(n):
            a[i] /= n

def multiply(a, b):
    """Product of two integer polynomials (coefficients below about 1e15 in the result)."""
    need = len(a) + len(b) - 1
    n = 1
    while n < need:
        n <<= 1                                         # power of two at least la + lb - 1
    fa = [complex(x) for x in a] + [0j] * (n - len(a))
    fb = [complex(x) for x in b] + [0j] * (n - len(b))
    fft(fa, False)
    fft(fb, False)
    fc = [x * y for x, y in zip(fa, fb)]                # pointwise product
    fft(fc, True)
    return [round(fc[i].real) for i in range(need)]     # exact integers after rounding
--cpp
// in-place FFT; the length must be a power of two. invert = true gives the inverse.
void fft(vector<complex<double>>& a, bool invert) {
    int n = a.size(), lg = floorLog2(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {                       // bit-reversal permutation
        int r = 0;
        for (int b = 0; b < lg; b++)
            if (getBit(i, b)) r = setBit(r, lg - 1 - b);
        if (i < r) swap(a[i], a[r]);
    }
    for (int len = 2; len <= n; len <<= 1) {
        int half = len / 2;
        double ang = 2 * acos(-1.0) / len * (invert ? -1 : 1);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i += len)
            for (int j = 0; j < half; j++) {
                complex<double> w(cos(ang * j), sin(ang * j));   // root of unity
                complex<double> u = a[i + j], v = a[i + j + half] * w;
                a[i + j] = u + v;
                a[i + j + half] = u - v;
            }
    }
    if (invert) for (auto& x : a) x /= n;
}

// product of two integer polynomials (coefficients below about 1e15 in the result)
vector<long long> multiply(const vector<long long>& a, const vector<long long>& b) {
    int need = a.size() + b.size() - 1, n = 1;
    while (n < need) n <<= 1;                           // power of two at least la + lb - 1
    vector<complex<double>> fa(a.begin(), a.end()), fb(b.begin(), b.end());
    fa.resize(n);
    fb.resize(n);
    fft(fa, false);
    fft(fb, false);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) fa[i] *= fb[i];         // pointwise product
    fft(fa, true);
    vector<long long> c(need);
    for (int i = 0; i < need; i++) c[i] = llround(fa[i].real());   // exact integers after rounding
    return c;
}
--go
// in-place FFT of (re, im); length must be a power of two. invert = true gives the inverse.
func fft(re, im []float64, invert bool) {
    n := len(re)
    lg := floorLog2(n)
    for i := 0; i < n; i++ { // bit-reversal permutation
        r := 0
        for b := 0; b < lg; b++ {
            if getBit(i, b) {
                r = setBit(r, lg-1-b)
            }
        }
        if i < r {
            re[i], re[r] = re[r], re[i]
            im[i], im[r] = im[r], im[i]
        }
    }
    for length := 2; length <= n; length <<= 1 {
        half := length / 2
        ang := 2 * math.Pi / float64(length)
        if invert {
            ang = -ang
        }
        for i := 0; i < n; i += length {
            for j := 0; j < half; j++ {
                wr, wi := math.Cos(ang*float64(j)), math.Sin(ang*float64(j)) // root of unity
                p, q := i+j, i+j+half
                xr := re[q]*wr - im[q]*wi // v = a[q] * w
                xi := re[q]*wi + im[q]*wr
                re[q], im[q] = re[p]-xr, im[p]-xi // a[p] - v
                re[p], im[p] = re[p]+xr, im[p]+xi // a[p] + v
            }
        }
    }
    if invert {
        for i := range re {
            re[i] /= float64(n)
            im[i] /= float64(n)
        }
    }
}

// product of two integer polynomials (coefficients below about 1e15 in the result)
func multiply(a, b []int) []int {
    need, n := len(a)+len(b)-1, 1
    for n < need { // power of two at least la + lb - 1
        n <<= 1
    }
    ar, ai, br, bi := make([]float64, n), make([]float64, n), make([]float64, n), make([]float64, n)
    for i, v := range a {
        ar[i] = float64(v)
    }
    for i, v := range b {
        br[i] = float64(v)
    }
    fft(ar, ai, false)
    fft(br, bi, false)
    for i := 0; i < n; i++ { // pointwise complex product
        r := ar[i]*br[i] - ai[i]*bi[i]
        ai[i] = ar[i]*bi[i] + ai[i]*br[i]
        ar[i] = r
    }
    fft(ar, ai, true)
    c := make([]int, need)
    for i := range c {
        c[i] = int(math.Round(ar[i])) // exact integers after rounding
    }
    return c
}
### ntt
--idea
The number theoretic transform (NTT) is the FFT done with whole numbers modulo a prime instead of complex numbers. It multiplies two polynomials exactly, with no rounding error, and gives the product coefficients modulo that prime. This is what contest problems usually want ("print the answer modulo 998244353"). The FFT needs roots of unity, special numbers `w` with `w^n = 1` where the powers `w^0 ... w^(n-1)` are all different. Complex numbers have them. Modulo a prime `p` they exist too, whenever `n` divides `p - 1`: take a primitive root `g` and use `w = g^((p-1)/n)`.
The prime 998244353 equals `119 * 2^23 + 1`, so it has roots of unity of every power-of-two order up to `2^23` (about 8 million), and `g = 3` is a primitive root. That is why it is so common. Tiny example with the prime 5 and `n = 4`: the root of unity is 2 (powers 2, 4, 3, 1). Multiply `(1 + 2x)(3 + 4x)`: evaluate `[1, 2, 0, 0]` at `1, 2, 4, 3` to get `[3, 0, 4, 2]`; `[3, 4, 0, 0]` gives `[2, 1, 4, 0]`; the pointwise product is `[1, 0, 1, 0]`. The inverse transform (roots `3 = 2^-1`, then multiply by `4 = 4^-1`) gives `[3, 0, 3, 0]`, which is `[3, 10, 8, 0]` modulo 5. Correct.
--build
1. Choose the prime `MOD = 998244353` and primitive root `G = 3`. All inputs must be reduced into `0..MOD-1`. Products of two residues are below `MOD^2`, about `10^18`, which fits in a signed 64-bit integer.
2. Size: the product of lengths `la` and `lb` has `la + lb - 1` coefficients. Take `n`, the smallest power of two at least that big (at most `2^23` for this prime). Pad both arrays with zeros.
3. Bit reversal: compute `rev[i] = (rev[i >> 1] >> 1) | ((i & 1) << (lg - 1))` with `lg = floorLog2(n)` (the `floorLog2` piece), and swap `a[i]` with `a[rev[i]]` when `i < rev[i]`. This puts the elements in the order that the divide and conquer recursion would leave them, so layers can be done in place.
4. Layers: for `len = 2, 4, ..., n`: the root for this layer is `w = G^((MOD-1)/len)` computed by fast power. For every block of `len` entries and every `j < len / 2`: `u = a[i + j]`, `v = a[i + j + len/2] * w^j mod MOD`; set `a[i + j] = u + v` and `a[i + j + len/2] = u - v` (both mod `MOD`). This is the evaluation step: even-index and odd-index halves are combined using `w^(len/2) = -1`.
5. Inverse transform: run the same layers with `w` replaced by its modular inverse (`w^(MOD-2)`), then multiply each entry by `n^(-1) mod MOD`. This is valid because the inverse of the evaluation matrix is the matrix built from `w^(-1)`, divided by `n`.
6. Multiply: transform both arrays, multiply entry by entry modulo `MOD`, inverse transform the result, and keep the first `la + lb - 1` entries.
7. Other primes: any `p = c * 2^k + 1` works if you know a primitive root (for example 469762049 with root 3, 167772161 with root 3). For an arbitrary modulus, convolve under two or three such primes and combine with the Chinese remainder theorem.
--think
- Signal: "compute the product of two big polynomials (or the convolution of two arrays) modulo 998244353", counting problems whose DP transition is a convolution, "number of ways" formulas that are sums of products.
- Decide: FFT with doubles for real inputs or when exactness up to `1e15` is enough; NTT when the answer is modulo a friendly prime. With a different modulus, use multi-prime NTT.
- Neighbours: FFT (the same algorithm with complex roots), Karatsuba (simpler, slower), Walsh-Hadamard (XOR convolution), polynomial inverse, log and exp (built on NTT multiply), primitive roots (where `G` comes from), fast power (computes the roots).
- Check the length limit: the padded length must divide `MOD - 1`'s power of two part; for 998244353 that is `2^23`.
- Pitfalls: inputs not reduced (negative numbers or values above `MOD`); forgetting the `n^(-1)` factor after the inverse; the wrong direction of the inverse roots; array too short for the full product (circular wrap-around); using `int` for the products.
--cost
Time O(n log n) per transform, three transforms per product. Space O(n).
--uses
u-log2
--java
static final long MOD = 998244353L, G = 3;

// in-place NTT; a.length must be a power of two and entries in 0..MOD-1
static void ntt(long[] a, boolean invert) {
    int n = a.length;
    if (n == 1) return;
    int lg = floorLog2(n);
    int[] rev = new int[n];
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) rev[i] = (rev[i >> 1] >> 1) | ((i & 1) << (lg - 1));   // reversed bits
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (i < rev[i]) { long t = a[i]; a[i] = a[rev[i]]; a[rev[i]] = t; }
    for (int len = 2; len <= n; len <<= 1) {
        long w = powMod(G, (MOD - 1) / len, MOD);           // a primitive len-th root of unity
        if (invert) w = powMod(w, MOD - 2, MOD);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i += len) {
            long cur = 1;
            for (int j = 0; j < len / 2; j++) {
                long u = a[i + j], v = a[i + j + len / 2] * cur % MOD;
                a[i + j] = (u + v) % MOD;
                a[i + j + len / 2] = (u - v + MOD) % MOD;
                cur = cur * w % MOD;
            }
        }
    }
    if (invert) {
        long nInv = powMod(n, MOD - 2, MOD);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) a[i] = a[i] * nInv % MOD;
    }
}

// product of two polynomials modulo MOD
static long[] multiply(long[] a, long[] b) {
    int need = a.length + b.length - 1, n = 1;
    while (n < need) n <<= 1;                               // power of two at least la + lb - 1
    long[] fa = new long[n], fb = new long[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) fa[i] = ((a[i] % MOD) + MOD) % MOD;
    for (int i = 0; i < b.length; i++) fb[i] = ((b[i] % MOD) + MOD) % MOD;
    ntt(fa, false);
    ntt(fb, false);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) fa[i] = fa[i] * fb[i] % MOD;   // pointwise product
    ntt(fa, true);
    return Arrays.copyOf(fa, need);
}
--py
MOD = 998244353
G = 3

def ntt(a, invert):
    """In-place NTT; len(a) must be a power of two and entries in 0..MOD-1."""
    n = len(a)
    if n == 1:
        return
    lg = floor_log2(n)
    rev = [0] * n
    for i in range(1, n):
        rev[i] = (rev[i >> 1] >> 1) | ((i & 1) << (lg - 1))     # reversed bits
    for i in range(n):
        if i < rev[i]:
            a[i], a[rev[i]] = a[rev[i]], a[i]
    length = 2
    while length <= n:
        w = pow(G, (MOD - 1) // length, MOD)                    # a primitive len-th root of unity
        if invert:
            w = pow(w, MOD - 2, MOD)
        half = length // 2
        for i in range(0, n, length):
            cur = 1
            for j in range(half):
                u, v = a[i + j], a[i + j + half] * cur % MOD
                a[i + j] = (u + v) % MOD
                a[i + j + half] = (u - v) % MOD
                cur = cur * w % MOD
        length <<= 1
    if invert:
        n_inv = pow(n, MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(n):
            a[i] = a[i] * n_inv % MOD

def multiply(a, b):
    """Product of two polynomials modulo MOD."""
    need = len(a) + len(b) - 1
    n = 1
    while n < need:
        n <<= 1                                                 # power of two at least la + lb - 1
    fa = [x % MOD for x in a] + [0] * (n - len(a))
    fb = [x % MOD for x in b] + [0] * (n - len(b))
    ntt(fa, False)
    ntt(fb, False)
    fa = [x * y % MOD for x, y in zip(fa, fb)]                  # pointwise product
    ntt(fa, True)
    return fa[:need]
--cpp
const long long MOD = 998244353LL, G = 3;

// in-place NTT; a.size() must be a power of two and entries in 0..MOD-1
void ntt(vector<long long>& a, bool invert) {
    int n = a.size();
    if (n == 1) return;
    int lg = floorLog2(n);
    vector<int> rev(n, 0);
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) rev[i] = (rev[i >> 1] >> 1) | ((i & 1) << (lg - 1));   // reversed bits
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (i < rev[i]) swap(a[i], a[rev[i]]);
    for (int len = 2; len <= n; len <<= 1) {
        long long w = powMod(G, (MOD - 1) / len, MOD);          // a primitive len-th root of unity
        if (invert) w = powMod(w, MOD - 2, MOD);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i += len) {
            long long cur = 1;
            for (int j = 0; j < len / 2; j++) {
                long long u = a[i + j], v = a[i + j + len / 2] * cur % MOD;
                a[i + j] = (u + v) % MOD;
                a[i + j + len / 2] = (u - v + MOD) % MOD;
                cur = cur * w % MOD;
            }
        }
    }
    if (invert) {
        long long nInv = powMod(n, MOD - 2, MOD);
        for (auto& x : a) x = x * nInv % MOD;
    }
}

// product of two polynomials modulo MOD
vector<long long> multiply(const vector<long long>& a, const vector<long long>& b) {
    int need = a.size() + b.size() - 1, n = 1;
    while (n < need) n <<= 1;                                   // power of two at least la + lb - 1
    vector<long long> fa(n, 0), fb(n, 0);
    for (size_t i = 0; i < a.size(); i++) fa[i] = ((a[i] % MOD) + MOD) % MOD;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < b.size(); i++) fb[i] = ((b[i] % MOD) + MOD) % MOD;
    ntt(fa, false);
    ntt(fb, false);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) fa[i] = fa[i] * fb[i] % MOD;    // pointwise product
    ntt(fa, true);
    fa.resize(need);
    return fa;
}
--go
const MOD, G = 998244353, 3

// in-place NTT; len(a) must be a power of two and entries in 0..MOD-1
func ntt(a []int, invert bool) {
    n := len(a)
    if n == 1 {
        return
    }
    lg := floorLog2(n)
    rev := make([]int, n)
    for i := 1; i < n; i++ {
        rev[i] = rev[i>>1]>>1 | (i&1)<<(lg-1) // reversed bits
    }
    for i := range a {
        if i < rev[i] {
            a[i], a[rev[i]] = a[rev[i]], a[i]
        }
    }
    for length := 2; length <= n; length <<= 1 {
        w := powMod(G, (MOD-1)/length, MOD) // a primitive len-th root of unity
        if invert {
            w = powMod(w, MOD-2, MOD)
        }
        for i := 0; i < n; i += length {
            cur := 1
            for j := 0; j < length/2; j++ {
                u, v := a[i+j], a[i+j+length/2]*cur%MOD
                a[i+j] = (u + v) % MOD
                a[i+j+length/2] = (u - v + MOD) % MOD
                cur = cur * w % MOD
            }
        }
    }
    if invert {
        nInv := powMod(n, MOD-2, MOD)
        for i := range a {
            a[i] = a[i] * nInv % MOD
        }
    }
}

// product of two polynomials modulo MOD
func multiply(a, b []int) []int {
    need, n := len(a)+len(b)-1, 1
    for n < need { // power of two at least la + lb - 1
        n <<= 1
    }
    fa, fb := make([]int, n), make([]int, n)
    for i, v := range a {
        fa[i] = ((v % MOD) + MOD) % MOD
    }
    for i, v := range b {
        fb[i] = ((v % MOD) + MOD) % MOD
    }
    ntt(fa, false)
    ntt(fb, false)
    for i := range fa {
        fa[i] = fa[i] * fb[i] % MOD // pointwise product
    }
    ntt(fa, true)
    return fa[:need]
}
### polyops
--idea
With fast multiplication (NTT) in hand, many other operations on power series become fast too: the inverse `1 / A(x)`, the logarithm `ln A(x)`, and the exponential `exp A(x)`. A power series here is a polynomial viewed modulo `x^n`: only the first `n` coefficients matter. These operations power counting problems (generating functions of partitions, set partitions, trees) where the answer is a coefficient of a series built from simpler ones.
The master trick is Newton's iteration, which doubles the number of correct coefficients each round. For the inverse: if `B` is correct modulo `x^m` (`A * B = 1 mod x^m`), then `B' = B * (2 - A * B)` is correct modulo `x^(2m)`. Start from the single coefficient `1 / a0`. Example: `A = 1 - x`. `B = [1]`; round 1 (`m = 2`): `A*B = 1 - x`, `2 - A*B = 1 + x`, `B' = 1 + x`; round 2 (`m = 4`): `B'' = 1 + x + x^2 + x^3`. Indeed `1 / (1 - x) = 1 + x + x^2 + ...`. Log and exp then follow from calculus: `ln A = integral of (A' / A)`, and `exp` is the inverse of `ln`, again by Newton: `B' = B * (1 - ln B + A)`.
--build
1. Everything works modulo a prime `MOD = 998244353`, on arrays of coefficients, using the `multiply` of the NTT topic. `trunc(a, n)` cuts or zero-pads an array to exactly `n` coefficients.
2. Inverse `polyInverse(a, n)` (needs `a[0] != 0`): start `b = [modInverse(a[0])]` (the `modInverse` piece). While `m < n`: double `m`; compute `ab = trunc(multiply(trunc(a, m), b), m)`; form `t = 2 - ab` (negate every entry, then add 2 to the constant term); set `b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m)`. Finally `trunc(b, n)`.
3. Why it works: write `A*B = 1 + x^m * E`. Then `A * B * (2 - A*B) = (1 + x^m E)(1 - x^m E) = 1 - x^(2m) E^2`, which equals 1 modulo `x^(2m)`.
4. Derivative: `d[i] = a[i + 1] * (i + 1)`. Integral: `g[i + 1] = a[i] * inverse(i + 1)` (one `modInverse` call per coefficient), with `g[0] = 0`.
5. Logarithm `polyLog(a, n)` (needs `a[0] = 1`): compute `q = A' * A^(-1)` modulo `x^(n-1)`, then integrate and cut to `n` terms. This is valid because `d/dx ln A = A' / A`.
6. Exponential `polyExp(a, n)` (needs `a[0] = 0`): start `b = [1]`. While `m < n`: double `m`; compute `l = polyLog(trunc(b, m), m)`; form `t = A - l` plus 1 on the constant term; set `b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m)`. Why: if `ln B = A + E` where the error `E` is divisible by `x^m`, then `B * (1 - E) = B(1 + A - ln B)` is correct modulo `x^(2m)`.
7. Costs: each round doubles `m` and costs `O(m log m)`, so the total is `O(n log n)` for the inverse; log adds one multiply; exp calls log in every round, still `O(n log n)` overall with a larger constant.
8. Sanity checks: `multiply(A, polyInverse(A))` should be `1` modulo `x^n`; `polyExp(polyLog(A))` should return `A`; `exp(x)` should have coefficients `1 / k!`.
--think
- Signal: "compute the n-th coefficient of 1/(1 - x - x^2)" (use inverse), "the generating function of partitions or of labelled structures" (exp and log of simple series), "number of forests / set partitions", "power of a series" (`exp(k ln A)`).
- Decide the length: all operations work modulo `x^n`; choose `n` as the largest index you need plus one.
- Neighbours: NTT multiply (the engine), generating functions (the language), linear recurrences (a rational series `P/Q` can be computed by one inverse), Bell and Stirling numbers (exp of `e^x - 1`), Kitamasa (an alternative for one far term).
- Order matters: the constant term must be 1 for log, 0 for exp, and non-zero for the inverse. If a series has another constant term, factor it out first.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to truncate (the lengths double every multiply); modular inverse of small indices (`i + 1` up to `n` is fine as `n < MOD`); mixing mod-`x^n` with exact arithmetic; naive `O(n^2)` multiply makes everything too slow for `n` above about 5000.
--cost
Inverse, log, exp: O(n log n) with NTT. Space O(n).
--uses
u-modinv
--java
// MOD = 998244353 and multiply() come from the NTT topic

static long[] trunc(long[] a, int n) { return Arrays.copyOf(a, n); }   // cut or zero-pad to n entries

// 1 / A modulo x^n (needs a[0] != 0); uses multiply from the NTT topic
static long[] polyInverse(long[] a, int n) {
    long[] b = {modInverse(a[0], MOD)};
    for (int m = 1; m < n; ) {
        m <<= 1;
        long[] ab = trunc(multiply(trunc(a, m), b), m);                 // A * B modulo x^m
        long[] t = new long[m];
        for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) t[i] = (MOD - ab[i]) % MOD;
        t[0] = (t[0] + 2) % MOD;                                       // 2 - A*B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m);                                  // Newton step: B * (2 - A*B)
    }
    return trunc(b, n);
}

static long[] polyDerivative(long[] a) {
    long[] d = new long[Math.max(1, a.length - 1)];
    for (int i = 0; i + 1 < a.length; i++) d[i] = a[i + 1] * (i + 1) % MOD;
    return d;
}

static long[] polyIntegral(long[] a) {
    long[] g = new long[a.length + 1];
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) g[i + 1] = a[i] * modInverse(i + 1, MOD) % MOD;
    return g;
}

// ln A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 1)
static long[] polyLog(long[] a, int n) {
    if (n == 1) return new long[]{0};
    long[] q = multiply(polyDerivative(trunc(a, n)), polyInverse(a, n));   // A' / A
    return trunc(polyIntegral(trunc(q, n - 1)), n);
}

// exp A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 0)
static long[] polyExp(long[] a, int n) {
    long[] b = {1};
    for (int m = 1; m < n; ) {
        m <<= 1;
        long[] l = polyLog(trunc(b, m), m);                            // ln B modulo x^m
        long[] t = new long[m];
        for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) t[i] = ((i < a.length ? a[i] : 0) - l[i] % MOD + 2 * MOD) % MOD;
        t[0] = (t[0] + 1) % MOD;                                       // 1 + A - ln B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m);                                  // Newton step: B * (1 + A - ln B)
    }
    return trunc(b, n);
}
--py
# MOD = 998244353 and multiply() come from the NTT topic

def trunc(a, n):
    """Cut or zero-pad to n entries."""
    return (a + [0] * n)[:n]

def poly_inverse(a, n):
    """1 / A modulo x^n (needs a[0] != 0); uses multiply from the NTT topic."""
    b = [mod_inverse(a[0], MOD)]
    m = 1
    while m < n:
        m <<= 1
        ab = trunc(multiply(trunc(a, m), b), m)                        # A * B modulo x^m
        t = [(-x) % MOD for x in ab]
        t[0] = (t[0] + 2) % MOD                                        # 2 - A*B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m)                                   # Newton step: B * (2 - A*B)
    return trunc(b, n)

def poly_derivative(a):
    return [a[i + 1] * (i + 1) % MOD for i in range(len(a) - 1)] or [0]

def poly_integral(a):
    return [0] + [a[i] * mod_inverse(i + 1, MOD) % MOD for i in range(len(a))]

def poly_log(a, n):
    """ln A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 1)."""
    if n == 1:
        return [0]
    q = multiply(poly_derivative(trunc(a, n)), poly_inverse(a, n))     # A' / A
    return trunc(poly_integral(trunc(q, n - 1)), n)

def poly_exp(a, n):
    """exp A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 0)."""
    b = [1]
    m = 1
    while m < n:
        m <<= 1
        l = poly_log(trunc(b, m), m)                                   # ln B modulo x^m
        t = [((a[i] if i < len(a) else 0) - l[i]) % MOD for i in range(m)]
        t[0] = (t[0] + 1) % MOD                                        # 1 + A - ln B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m)                                   # Newton step: B * (1 + A - ln B)
    return trunc(b, n)
--cpp
// MOD = 998244353 and multiply() come from the NTT topic

vector<long long> trunc(vector<long long> a, int n) { a.resize(n, 0); return a; }   // cut or zero-pad

// 1 / A modulo x^n (needs a[0] != 0); uses multiply from the NTT topic
vector<long long> polyInverse(const vector<long long>& a, int n) {
    vector<long long> b = {modInverse(a[0], MOD)};
    for (int m = 1; m < n; ) {
        m <<= 1;
        vector<long long> ab = trunc(multiply(trunc(a, m), b), m);       // A * B modulo x^m
        vector<long long> t(m);
        for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) t[i] = (MOD - ab[i]) % MOD;
        t[0] = (t[0] + 2) % MOD;                                         // 2 - A*B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m);                                    // Newton step: B * (2 - A*B)
    }
    return trunc(b, n);
}

vector<long long> polyDerivative(const vector<long long>& a) {
    vector<long long> d(max<size_t>(1, a.size() - 1), 0);
    for (size_t i = 0; i + 1 < a.size(); i++) d[i] = a[i + 1] * (i + 1) % MOD;
    return d;
}

vector<long long> polyIntegral(const vector<long long>& a) {
    vector<long long> g(a.size() + 1, 0);
    for (size_t i = 0; i < a.size(); i++) g[i + 1] = a[i] * modInverse(i + 1, MOD) % MOD;
    return g;
}

// ln A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 1)
vector<long long> polyLog(const vector<long long>& a, int n) {
    if (n == 1) return {0};
    vector<long long> q = multiply(polyDerivative(trunc(a, n)), polyInverse(a, n));   // A' / A
    return trunc(polyIntegral(trunc(q, n - 1)), n);
}

// exp A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 0)
vector<long long> polyExp(const vector<long long>& a, int n) {
    vector<long long> b = {1};
    for (int m = 1; m < n; ) {
        m <<= 1;
        vector<long long> l = polyLog(trunc(b, m), m);                   // ln B modulo x^m
        vector<long long> t(m);
        for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) t[i] = ((i < (int)a.size() ? a[i] : 0) - l[i] % MOD + 2 * MOD) % MOD;
        t[0] = (t[0] + 1) % MOD;                                         // 1 + A - ln B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m);                                    // Newton step: B * (1 + A - ln B)
    }
    return trunc(b, n);
}
--go
// MOD = 998244353 and multiply() come from the NTT topic

func trunc(a []int, n int) []int { // cut or zero-pad to n entries
    r := make([]int, n)
    copy(r, a)
    return r
}

// 1 / A modulo x^n (needs a[0] != 0); uses multiply from the NTT topic
func polyInverse(a []int, n int) []int {
    b := []int{modInverse(a[0], MOD)}
    for m := 1; m < n; {
        m <<= 1
        ab := trunc(multiply(trunc(a, m), b), m) // A * B modulo x^m
        t := make([]int, m)
        for i := range t {
            t[i] = (MOD - ab[i]) % MOD
        }
        t[0] = (t[0] + 2) % MOD         // 2 - A*B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m)    // Newton step: B * (2 - A*B)
    }
    return trunc(b, n)
}

func polyDerivative(a []int) []int {
    d := make([]int, max(1, len(a)-1))
    for i := 0; i+1 < len(a); i++ {
        d[i] = a[i+1] * (i + 1) % MOD
    }
    return d
}

func polyIntegral(a []int) []int {
    g := make([]int, len(a)+1)
    for i, v := range a {
        g[i+1] = v * modInverse(i+1, MOD) % MOD
    }
    return g
}

// ln A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 1)
func polyLog(a []int, n int) []int {
    if n == 1 {
        return []int{0}
    }
    q := multiply(polyDerivative(trunc(a, n)), polyInverse(a, n)) // A' / A
    return trunc(polyIntegral(trunc(q, n-1)), n)
}

// exp A modulo x^n (needs a[0] = 0)
func polyExp(a []int, n int) []int {
    b := []int{1}
    for m := 1; m < n; {
        m <<= 1
        l := polyLog(trunc(b, m), m) // ln B modulo x^m
        t := make([]int, m)
        for i := range t {
            ai := 0
            if i < len(a) {
                ai = a[i]
            }
            t[i] = (ai - l[i]%MOD + 2*MOD) % MOD
        }
        t[0] = (t[0] + 1) % MOD      // 1 + A - ln B
        b = trunc(multiply(b, t), m) // Newton step: B * (1 + A - ln B)
    }
    return trunc(b, n)
}
### floorsum
--idea
The floor sum is `sum over i = 0..n-1 of floor((a*i + b) / m)`: add up the integer parts of points on a line. It appears when counting lattice points (points with whole-number coordinates) under a line, in modular and number theory sums, and in geometry. A direct loop is `O(n)`; a trick that mirrors the Euclidean algorithm for gcd does it in `O(log m)`, so `n` can be 10^9 or more.
Example: `n = 4, m = 3, a = 2, b = 1`: the values `floor((2i + 1)/3)` for `i = 0..3` are `0, 1, 1, 2`, total 4. The method: (1) reduce a large slope: if `a >= m` write `a = (a / m) * m + (a mod m)`, which contributes `(a/m) * (0 + 1 + ... + (n-1)) = (a/m) * n(n-1)/2` and leaves `a mod m`. (2) Reduce the offset the same way: if `b >= m` add `n * (b / m)` and replace `b` by `b mod m`. (3) Now `a < m` and `b < m`, so the line is flatter than 1 and the sum counts lattice points under it. Count them by rows instead of columns: this is again a floor sum with the roles of `a` and `m` swapped and a much smaller size. Repeating shrinks the numbers like the Euclidean algorithm.
--build
1. Define `floorSum(n, m, a, b) = sum over i = 0..n-1 of floor((a*i + b) / m)`, with `a, b >= 0`, `m > 0`.
2. If `a >= m`: add `(a / m) * (n * (n - 1) / 2)` to the answer and set `a = a mod m`. Valid because `floor((a*i + b)/m) = (a/m) * i + floor(((a mod m)*i + b)/m)`.
3. If `b >= m`: add `(b / m) * n` and set `b = b mod m`, by the same split of the constant term.
4. Let `y = a * n + b`. If `y < m`, every term is 0: return the answer so far.
5. Otherwise the number of lattice points `(i, j)` with `1 <= j` and `m*j <= a*i + b`, `0 <= i < n` is what remains. Counting by `j` instead: there are `y / m` rows. Row `j` holds the points with `i >= (m*j - b) / a`, which makes the answer the floor sum over `j` with slope `m / a`. After shifting by the constant, that equals `floorSum(y / m, a, m, y mod m)`: new size `y / m`, new modulus `a`, new slope `m`, new offset `y mod m`.
6. Add the result of that recursive call. Each recursion swaps the roles of `a` and `m` and reduces one of them modulo the other, as the Euclidean algorithm does, so the depth is `O(log m)`.
7. Overflow: `a * n + b` can reach about `m * n`. Use `long` when `n * m` is below `9e18`, and `__int128` (or an unsigned type) beyond that.
--think
- Signal: "sum of floor((a*i + b)/m)", "number of lattice points under a line", "sum of `(a*i + b) mod m`" (since `(x mod m) = x - m*floor(x/m)`), counting multiples in arithmetic progressions, geometric counting on grids.
- Decide: if `n` is up to about 1e7, a direct loop is simplest; for `n` up to 1e18 use this. If a similar sum includes `i * floor(...)` or `floor(...)^2`, an extended version carries three quantities.
- Neighbours: gcd (the same shrinking pattern), harmonic blocks (sums of `floor(n / i)` with varying divisor, a different shape), Euclid-like recursion on lattice points, Pick's theorem and counting integer points in polygons.
- Writing `sum of (a*i + b) mod m` as `sum of (a*i + b) - m * floorSum(...)` is the most common use.
- Pitfalls: negative `a` or `b` (shift into non-negative first); `n * (n - 1) / 2` overflowing; wrong order of arguments in the recursive call; not stopping when `y < m`.
--cost
Time O(log m). Space O(log m) for the recursion.
--java
// sum over i = 0..n-1 of floor((a*i + b) / m), for a, b >= 0 and m > 0
static long floorSum(long n, long m, long a, long b) {
    long ans = 0;
    if (a >= m) { ans += (a / m) * (n * (n - 1) / 2); a %= m; }   // split off the whole-number slope
    if (b >= m) { ans += (b / m) * n; b %= m; }                    // split off the whole-number offset
    long y = a * n + b;
    if (y < m) return ans;                                         // every remaining term is 0
    return ans + floorSum(y / m, a, m, y % m);                     // count the lattice points by rows
}
--py
def floor_sum(n, m, a, b):
    """Sum over i = 0..n-1 of (a*i + b) // m, for a, b >= 0 and m > 0."""
    ans = 0
    if a >= m:
        ans += (a // m) * (n * (n - 1) // 2)       # split off the whole-number slope
        a %= m
    if b >= m:
        ans += (b // m) * n                        # split off the whole-number offset
        b %= m
    y = a * n + b
    if y < m:
        return ans                                 # every remaining term is 0
    return ans + floor_sum(y // m, a, m, y % m)    # count the lattice points by rows
--cpp
// sum over i = 0..n-1 of floor((a*i + b) / m), for a, b >= 0 and m > 0
long long floorSum(long long n, long long m, long long a, long long b) {
    long long ans = 0;
    if (a >= m) { ans += (a / m) * (n * (n - 1) / 2); a %= m; }   // split off the whole-number slope
    if (b >= m) { ans += (b / m) * n; b %= m; }                    // split off the whole-number offset
    long long y = a * n + b;
    if (y < m) return ans;                                         // every remaining term is 0
    return ans + floorSum(y / m, a, m, y % m);                     // count the lattice points by rows
}
--go
// sum over i = 0..n-1 of floor((a*i + b) / m), for a, b >= 0 and m > 0
func floorSum(n, m, a, b int) int {
    ans := 0
    if a >= m { // split off the whole-number slope
        ans += (a / m) * (n * (n - 1) / 2)
        a %= m
    }
    if b >= m { // split off the whole-number offset
        ans += (b / m) * n
        b %= m
    }
    y := a*n + b
    if y < m { // every remaining term is 0
        return ans
    }
    return ans + floorSum(y/m, a, m, y%m) // count the lattice points by rows
}
### min25
--idea
Counting the primes up to a huge `n` (like `10^11`) is possible without listing them. This lesson shows the first phase of the min_25 sieve, usually called Lucy's algorithm: it computes `pi(v)`, the number of primes up to `v`, for every `v` of the form `floor(n / i)` in `O(n^(3/4))` time. There are only about `2 * sqrt(n)` such values, and they are closed under the operation we need: `floor(floor(n / i) / p) = floor(n / (i * p))`.
Start by pretending every number from 2 to `v` is prime: `S(v) = v - 1`. Then go through primes `p = 2, 3, 5, ...` up to `sqrt(n)` and cross out their composites. Crossing out multiples of `p` whose smallest prime factor is `p` removes `S(v / p) - S(p - 1)` numbers from the count at `v`: the numbers `p * k` with `k` up to `v / p` and `k` not having a prime factor below `p` (the count `S(v/p)` is `k` still alive, and `S(p-1)` removes primes below `p`). Example `n = 10`: values `10, 5, 3, 2, 1` with `S = 9, 4, 2, 1, 0`. Prime 2: `S(10) -= S(5) - S(1) = 4` gives 5; `S(5) -= S(2) - S(1) = 1` gives 3. Prime 3: `S(10) -= S(3) - S(2) = 1` gives 4. So `pi(10) = 4`: 2, 3, 5, 7.
--build
1. Let `r = floor(sqrt(n))`. Store two arrays: `lo[v]` for `v = 0..r` (the count `S(v)` for small `v`) and `hi[i]` for `i = 1..r` (the count `S(n / i)` for large values). Start `lo[v] = v - 1` (with `lo[0] = 0`) and `hi[i] = n / i - 1`.
2. Get the primes up to `r` from the `buildSpf` piece: `p` is prime exactly when `spf[p] == p`.
3. For each prime `p` (in increasing order) with `p * p <= n`: let `pc = lo[p - 1]` (the number of primes below `p`).
4. Update the large values: for `i = 1..min(r, n / (p * p))`, let `d = i * p`. The quantity `S(n / d)` is `hi[d]` if `d <= r`, else it is `lo[n / d]` (because `n / d` is then at most `r`). Do `hi[i] -= S(n / d) - pc`.
5. Update the small values, from large to small (so you read old values): for `v = r` down to `p * p`: `lo[v] -= lo[v / p] - pc`.
6. Order matters: the large values are updated first and use `lo` values that are not yet changed for this prime. The small values go downward because `v / p < v`.
7. After all primes up to `r`, `hi[1] = pi(n)`. Also `lo[v] = pi(v)` for every `v <= r`.
8. Phase two of the min_25 sieve (not shown) reuses the same value set to sum multiplicative functions over all numbers up to `n`.
--think
- Signal: "count primes up to 1e10 to 1e12", "sum of primes up to n", "sum of a multiplicative function up to n", problems where `n` is too big to sieve but `sqrt(n)` is small.
- Decide: below about `1e8` a plain sieve is simpler. For `n` between `1e9` and `1e12`, Lucy's algorithm needs `n^(3/4)` operations (a few hundred million at `1e12`, and a few seconds). Beyond `1e13` use the Meissel-Lehmer variants.
- Neighbours: sieve of Eratosthenes (small `n`), segmented sieve (windows), Dirichlet convolution and harmonic blocks (the structure of the value set), Mobius inversion, primality testing (for single numbers).
- The same recurrence counts primes weighted by any completely multiplicative function (for example the sum of primes uses `S(v) = v(v+1)/2 - 1` and multiplies by `p`).
- Pitfalls: indexing the large values by `i` and not by `n / i`; updating small values upward (reads new data); forgetting `lo[p - 1]` as the correction `pc`; `n / d` when `d > r` must use the `lo` table; overflow in the sum-of-primes variants.
--cost
Time O(n^(3/4)). Space O(sqrt(n)).
--uses
u-spf
--java
// pi(n): the number of primes up to n (n up to about 1e12)
static long primeCount(long n) {
    if (n < 2) return 0;
    int r = (int) Math.sqrt((double) n);
    while ((long) r * r > n) r--;
    while ((long) (r + 1) * (r + 1) <= n) r++;
    long[] lo = new long[r + 2], hi = new long[r + 2];
    for (int v = 1; v <= r; v++) { lo[v] = v - 1; hi[v] = n / v - 1; }
    int[] spf = buildSpf(r);                                   // smallest prime factors (piece)
    for (int p = 2; p <= r; p++) {
        if (spf[p] != p) continue;                             // only primes
        long pc = lo[p - 1], p2 = (long) p * p;
        if (p2 > n) break;
        long lim = Math.min(r, n / p2);
        for (int i = 1; i <= lim; i++) {
            long d = (long) i * p;                             // S(n / d) comes from hi or lo
            long s = d <= r ? hi[(int) d] : lo[(int) (n / d)];
            hi[i] -= s - pc;
        }
        for (int v = r; v >= p2; v--) lo[v] -= lo[v / p] - pc;  // downward: read old values
    }
    return hi[1];
}
--py
from math import isqrt

def prime_count(n):
    """pi(n): the number of primes up to n (n up to about 1e12 in compiled languages)."""
    if n < 2:
        return 0
    r = isqrt(n)
    lo = [0] + [v - 1 for v in range(1, r + 1)]
    hi = [0] + [n // v - 1 for v in range(1, r + 1)]
    spf = build_spf(r)                                         # smallest prime factors (piece)
    for p in range(2, r + 1):
        if spf[p] != p:
            continue                                           # only primes
        pc, p2 = lo[p - 1], p * p
        if p2 > n:
            break
        for i in range(1, min(r, n // p2) + 1):
            d = i * p                                          # S(n / d) comes from hi or lo
            s = hi[d] if d <= r else lo[n // d]
            hi[i] -= s - pc
        for v in range(r, p2 - 1, -1):                         # downward: read old values
            lo[v] -= lo[v // p] - pc
    return hi[1]
--cpp
// pi(n): the number of primes up to n (n up to about 1e12)
long long primeCount(long long n) {
    if (n < 2) return 0;
    long long r = (long long)sqrtl((long double)n);
    while (r * r > n) r--;
    while ((r + 1) * (r + 1) <= n) r++;
    vector<long long> lo(r + 2, 0), hi(r + 2, 0);
    for (long long v = 1; v <= r; v++) { lo[v] = v - 1; hi[v] = n / v - 1; }
    vector<int> spf = buildSpf((int)r);                        // smallest prime factors (piece)
    for (long long p = 2; p <= r; p++) {
        if (spf[p] != p) continue;                             // only primes
        long long pc = lo[p - 1], p2 = p * p;
        if (p2 > n) break;
        long long lim = min(r, n / p2);
        for (long long i = 1; i <= lim; i++) {
            long long d = i * p;                               // S(n / d) comes from hi or lo
            long long s = d <= r ? hi[d] : lo[n / d];
            hi[i] -= s - pc;
        }
        for (long long v = r; v >= p2; v--) lo[v] -= lo[v / p] - pc;   // downward: read old values
    }
    return hi[1];
}
--go
// pi(n): the number of primes up to n (n up to about 1e12)
func primeCount(n int) int {
    if n < 2 {
        return 0
    }
    r := int(math.Sqrt(float64(n)))
    for r*r > n {
        r--
    }
    for (r+1)*(r+1) <= n {
        r++
    }
    lo, hi := make([]int, r+2), make([]int, r+2)
    for v := 1; v <= r; v++ {
        lo[v], hi[v] = v-1, n/v-1
    }
    spf := buildSpf(r) // smallest prime factors (piece)
    for p := 2; p <= r; p++ {
        if spf[p] != p { // only primes
            continue
        }
        pc, p2 := lo[p-1], p*p
        if p2 > n {
            break
        }
        lim := min(r, n/p2)
        for i := 1; i <= lim; i++ {
            d := i * p // S(n / d) comes from hi or lo
            var s int
            if d <= r {
                s = hi[d]
            } else {
                s = lo[n/d]
            }
            hi[i] -= s - pc
        }
        for v := r; v >= p2; v-- { // downward: read old values
            lo[v] -= lo[v/p] - pc
        }
    }
    return hi[1]
}
### randwalk
--idea
A random walk is a path built from random steps: at every moment the walker moves left or right with some probability. Classic question: a gambler starts with `i` coins and bets one coin per round on a fair coin flip, stopping at 0 (ruin) or at `N` (target). What is the chance of reaching `N`, and how many rounds does it take on average? Walking on the positions `0..N` with two absorbing ends (the walk stops there) is the "gambler's ruin" model, and its answers are neat.
For the fair walk (`p = 1/2` to the right): the chance of reaching `N` before 0 from `i` is `i / N`, and the expected number of steps is `i * (N - i)`. Example: `N = 4, i = 1`: win probability `1/4`, expected steps `1 * 3 = 3`. Check by an equation: let `E(i)` be the expected steps from `i`. Then `E(i) = 1 + (E(i-1) + E(i+1)) / 2` with `E(0) = E(N) = 0`. For `N = 4`: `E(1) = E(3)` by symmetry, `E(2) = 1 + E(1)`, and `E(1) = 1 + E(2)/2 = 1 + (1 + E(1))/2`, giving `E(1) = 3`. For an unfair walk (probability `p` right, `q = 1 - p` left, `r = q/p`), the win chance is `(1 - r^i) / (1 - r^N)`.
--build
1. State: the position `i` in `0..N`. The two end positions are absorbing: `E(0) = E(N) = 0` for the expected time, and win probability 0 and 1 there.
2. Win probability `W(i)`: it satisfies `W(i) = p * W(i+1) + q * W(i-1)`, `W(0) = 0`, `W(N) = 1`. The solution: for `p = 1/2`, `W(i) = i / N`; otherwise `W(i) = (1 - r^i) / (1 - r^N)` with `r = q / p`.
3. Expected duration `E(i)`: it satisfies `E(i) = 1 + p * E(i+1) + q * E(i-1)`. The solution: for `p = 1/2`, `E(i) = i (N - i)`; otherwise `E(i) = i / (q - p) - (N / (q - p)) * (1 - r^i) / (1 - r^N)`.
4. General method (works for any walk on a line, with different probabilities at each position): the equations `-q * E(i-1) + E(i) - p * E(i+1) = 1` for `i = 1..N-1` form a tridiagonal system (only three diagonals are non-zero). Solve it by the Thomas algorithm: a forward sweep that eliminates the lower diagonal, then back substitution. It costs `O(N)`.
5. Forward sweep: for `i = 1..N-1` keep modified coefficients `cp[i]` (upper diagonal after elimination) and `dp[i]` (right side after elimination): `cp[i] = c / (b - a * cp[i-1])` and `dp[i] = (d - a * dp[i-1]) / (b - a * cp[i-1])`, with `a = -q`, `b = 1`, `c = -p`, `d = 1`.
6. Back substitution: `E(N-1) = dp[N-1]`, then `E(i) = dp[i] - cp[i] * E(i+1)` going down.
7. Check the closed form against the numeric solution on small cases.
--think
- Signal: "expected number of steps until the walker hits", "probability that the walker reaches X before Y", gambler's ruin, a token moving on a line or cycle, a drunk at a bar, an ant on a stick.
- Decide whether there is a closed form (one dimensional, uniform probabilities) or you need a linear system. For general graphs the expected hitting times satisfy one equation per vertex: solve with Gaussian elimination (Markov chain methods).
- Neighbours: expected value (setting up `E(state)`), Markov chains (the general framework), Gaussian elimination (small graphs), linearity of expectation (when summing indicator pieces is simpler), DP for walks with bounded steps.
- Symmetric walks are special: the expected time grows like the square of the distance (`i * (N - i)`), a signature of diffusion.
- Pitfalls: `p = 1/2` makes `r = 1`, so the general formula divides by zero (use the special case); mixing up the absorbing ends; forgetting the `+ 1` cost of each step; using the formula when the ends are reflecting rather than absorbing.
--cost
Closed forms O(1) (or O(log) for powers). Thomas algorithm O(N) time and space.
--java
// probability of reaching N before 0, starting at i, stepping right with probability p
static double winProbability(int i, int N, double p) {
    if (Math.abs(p - 0.5) < 1e-12) return (double) i / N;
    double r = (1 - p) / p;
    return (1 - Math.pow(r, i)) / (1 - Math.pow(r, N));
}

// expected number of steps until the walk hits 0 or N, closed form
static double expectedDuration(int i, int N, double p) {
    if (Math.abs(p - 0.5) < 1e-12) return (double) i * (N - i);
    double q = 1 - p, r = q / p;
    return i / (q - p) - (N / (q - p)) * (1 - Math.pow(r, i)) / (1 - Math.pow(r, N));
}

// the same expectation for every start 0..N by the Thomas algorithm on the tridiagonal system
static double[] expectedDurations(int N, double p) {
    double q = 1 - p;
    double[] E = new double[N + 1], cp = new double[N], dp = new double[N];
    for (int i = 1; i < N; i++) {                          // forward sweep: a = -q, b = 1, c = -p, d = 1
        double denom = 1 + q * (i > 1 ? cp[i - 1] : 0);
        cp[i] = -p / denom;
        dp[i] = (1 + q * (i > 1 ? dp[i - 1] : 0)) / denom;
    }
    for (int i = N - 1; i >= 1; i--)                       // back substitution
        E[i] = dp[i] - cp[i] * E[i + 1];
    return E;
}
--py
def win_probability(i, N, p):
    """Probability of reaching N before 0, starting at i, stepping right with probability p."""
    if abs(p - 0.5) < 1e-12:
        return i / N
    r = (1 - p) / p
    return (1 - r ** i) / (1 - r ** N)

def expected_duration(i, N, p):
    """Expected number of steps until the walk hits 0 or N, closed form."""
    if abs(p - 0.5) < 1e-12:
        return i * (N - i)
    q = 1 - p
    r = q / p
    return i / (q - p) - (N / (q - p)) * (1 - r ** i) / (1 - r ** N)

def expected_durations(N, p):
    """The same expectation for every start 0..N by the Thomas algorithm on the tridiagonal system."""
    q = 1 - p
    E = [0.0] * (N + 1)
    cp = [0.0] * N
    dp = [0.0] * N
    for i in range(1, N):                                 # forward sweep: a = -q, b = 1, c = -p, d = 1
        denom = 1 + q * (cp[i - 1] if i > 1 else 0)
        cp[i] = -p / denom
        dp[i] = (1 + q * (dp[i - 1] if i > 1 else 0)) / denom
    for i in range(N - 1, 0, -1):                         # back substitution
        E[i] = dp[i] - cp[i] * E[i + 1]
    return E
--cpp
// probability of reaching N before 0, starting at i, stepping right with probability p
double winProbability(int i, int N, double p) {
    if (fabs(p - 0.5) < 1e-12) return (double)i / N;
    double r = (1 - p) / p;
    return (1 - pow(r, i)) / (1 - pow(r, N));
}

// expected number of steps until the walk hits 0 or N, closed form
double expectedDuration(int i, int N, double p) {
    if (fabs(p - 0.5) < 1e-12) return (double)i * (N - i);
    double q = 1 - p, r = q / p;
    return i / (q - p) - (N / (q - p)) * (1 - pow(r, i)) / (1 - pow(r, N));
}

// the same expectation for every start 0..N by the Thomas algorithm on the tridiagonal system
vector<double> expectedDurations(int N, double p) {
    double q = 1 - p;
    vector<double> E(N + 1, 0.0), cp(N, 0.0), dp(N, 0.0);
    for (int i = 1; i < N; i++) {                          // forward sweep: a = -q, b = 1, c = -p, d = 1
        double denom = 1 + q * (i > 1 ? cp[i - 1] : 0);
        cp[i] = -p / denom;
        dp[i] = (1 + q * (i > 1 ? dp[i - 1] : 0)) / denom;
    }
    for (int i = N - 1; i >= 1; i--)                       // back substitution
        E[i] = dp[i] - cp[i] * E[i + 1];
    return E;
}
--go
// probability of reaching N before 0, starting at i, stepping right with probability p
func winProbability(i, N int, p float64) float64 {
    if math.Abs(p-0.5) < 1e-12 {
        return float64(i) / float64(N)
    }
    r := (1 - p) / p
    return (1 - math.Pow(r, float64(i))) / (1 - math.Pow(r, float64(N)))
}

// expected number of steps until the walk hits 0 or N, closed form
func expectedDuration(i, N int, p float64) float64 {
    if math.Abs(p-0.5) < 1e-12 {
        return float64(i * (N - i))
    }
    q := 1 - p
    r := q / p
    return float64(i)/(q-p) - (float64(N)/(q-p))*(1-math.Pow(r, float64(i)))/(1-math.Pow(r, float64(N)))
}

// the same expectation for every start 0..N by the Thomas algorithm on the tridiagonal system
func expectedDurations(N int, p float64) []float64 {
    q := 1 - p
    E := make([]float64, N+1)
    cp, dp := make([]float64, N), make([]float64, N)
    for i := 1; i < N; i++ { // forward sweep: a = -q, b = 1, c = -p, d = 1
        pc, pd := 0.0, 0.0
        if i > 1 {
            pc, pd = cp[i-1], dp[i-1]
        }
        denom := 1 + q*pc
        cp[i] = -p / denom
        dp[i] = (1 + q*pd) / denom
    }
    for i := N - 1; i >= 1; i-- { // back substitution
        E[i] = dp[i] - cp[i]*E[i+1]
    }
    return E
}
### markov
--idea
A Markov chain is a system that hops between a fixed set of states, where the chance of the next state depends only on the current state (not on how you got there). Weather is the usual picture: if today is sunny, tomorrow is sunny with chance 0.9 and rainy with 0.1; if today is rainy, tomorrow is sunny with 0.5 and rainy with 0.5. The chances live in a transition matrix `P`, where `P[i][j]` is the chance of going from state `i` to state `j` and every row sums to 1.
If `v` is a row vector of current probabilities over the states, then `v * P` is the distribution one step later, and `v * P^n` is the distribution after `n` steps. So "where is the chain after a billion steps" is a matrix power, the same repeated squaring used for recurrences. Many chains settle down: the stationary distribution `pi` satisfies `pi * P = pi`. For the weather chain, `pi_S = 0.9 pi_S + 0.5 pi_R` gives `pi_S = 5 pi_R`, so `pi = (5/6, 1/6)`: in the long run five days in six are sunny, whatever the start.
--build
1. List the states and fill the matrix `P` with the one-step chances. Check that each row adds up to 1.
2. Exact answers modulo a prime: when a problem says "give the probability as `p * q^(-1) mod M`", replace each chance `a / b` by `a * b^(-1) mod M` (a modular inverse), and do every operation modulo `M`. The rules of matrices still hold because the arithmetic is a field.
3. Distribution after `steps` steps: compute `Q = P^steps` with the `matPow` piece (repeated squaring, `O(k^3 log steps)`), then the new distribution is `v * Q`: `result[j] = sum over i of v[i] * Q[i][j]`. This is `stateAfter`.
4. Stationary distribution (real numbers): start from any distribution (for example uniform) and repeat `v = v * P` many times. For a chain that is irreducible (every state reachable from every other) and aperiodic (not locked in a fixed cycle), `v` converges to the unique `pi`. This is `stationaryDistribution` (power iteration).
5. Alternative for the stationary distribution: solve the linear system `pi * (P - I) = 0` together with `sum of pi = 1` by Gaussian elimination.
6. Absorbing chains (some states are traps): the probability of ending in each trap and the expected time to be absorbed come from the fundamental matrix `(I - Q)^(-1)`, where `Q` is the transition among non-trap states.
--think
- Signal: "after n moves, what is the probability of being in state X", "random walk on a graph", "in the long run", "expected number of steps until reaching a state" (an absorbing chain), board games with dice, queue lengths.
- Decide the state: it must contain everything the next step depends on. Then count the states `k`; matrix power is fine up to a few hundred states.
- Neighbours: matrix exponentiation (the engine for huge step counts), Gaussian elimination (stationary and absorbing solutions), expected value (a chain with costs), random walks on a line (a special chain with closed forms), DP over steps (`O(steps * k^2)` when the step count is small).
- Distinguish: finite step count with huge `steps` means matrix power; infinite horizon means stationary distribution or linear equations.
- Pitfalls: a row that does not sum to 1; row versus column vector convention (`v * P` with row vectors); periodic chains that never converge in power iteration; forgetting the modular inverse when working modulo a prime; a non-prime modulus.
--cost
Matrix power O(k^3 log steps). Power iteration O(k^2) per iteration. Space O(k^2).
--uses
u-matpow
--java
// distribution after `steps` steps: v * P^steps, all entries modulo M (chances stored as a * b^-1 mod M)
static long[] stateAfter(long[][] P, long[] v, long steps, long M) {
    long[][] Q = matPow(P, steps, M);                // repeated squaring (piece)
    int k = v.length;
    long[] res = new long[k];
    for (int j = 0; j < k; j++)
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++)
            res[j] = (res[j] + v[i] * Q[i][j]) % M;  // row vector times matrix
    return res;
}

// stationary distribution of an irreducible, aperiodic chain by power iteration
static double[] stationaryDistribution(double[][] P, int iterations) {
    int k = P.length;
    double[] v = new double[k];
    java.util.Arrays.fill(v, 1.0 / k);               // start from the uniform distribution
    for (int it = 0; it < iterations; it++) {
        double[] next = new double[k];
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++)
            for (int j = 0; j < k; j++) next[j] += v[i] * P[i][j];   // one step: v * P
        v = next;
    }
    return v;
}
--py
def state_after(P, v, steps, M):
    """Distribution after `steps` steps: v * P**steps, all entries modulo M (chances stored as a * b^-1 mod M)."""
    Q = mat_pow(P, steps, M)                         # repeated squaring (piece)
    k = len(v)
    return [sum(v[i] * Q[i][j] for i in range(k)) % M for j in range(k)]   # row vector times matrix

def stationary_distribution(P, iterations):
    """Stationary distribution of an irreducible, aperiodic chain by power iteration."""
    k = len(P)
    v = [1.0 / k] * k                                # start from the uniform distribution
    for _ in range(iterations):
        v = [sum(v[i] * P[i][j] for i in range(k)) for j in range(k)]   # one step: v * P
    return v
--cpp
// distribution after `steps` steps: v * P^steps, all entries modulo M (chances stored as a * b^-1 mod M)
vector<long long> stateAfter(const Mat& P, const vector<long long>& v, long long steps, long long M) {
    Mat Q = matPow(P, steps, M);                     // repeated squaring (piece)
    int k = v.size();
    vector<long long> res(k, 0);
    for (int j = 0; j < k; j++)
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++)
            res[j] = (res[j] + v[i] * Q[i][j]) % M;  // row vector times matrix
    return res;
}

// stationary distribution of an irreducible, aperiodic chain by power iteration
vector<double> stationaryDistribution(const vector<vector<double>>& P, int iterations) {
    int k = P.size();
    vector<double> v(k, 1.0 / k);                    // start from the uniform distribution
    for (int it = 0; it < iterations; it++) {
        vector<double> next(k, 0.0);
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++)
            for (int j = 0; j < k; j++) next[j] += v[i] * P[i][j];   // one step: v * P
        v = next;
    }
    return v;
}
--go
// distribution after steps steps: v * P^steps, all entries modulo M (chances stored as a * b^-1 mod M)
func stateAfter(P [][]int, v []int, steps, M int) []int {
    Q := matPow(P, steps, M) // repeated squaring (piece)
    k := len(v)
    res := make([]int, k)
    for j := 0; j < k; j++ {
        for i := 0; i < k; i++ {
            res[j] = (res[j] + v[i]*Q[i][j]) % M // row vector times matrix
        }
    }
    return res
}

// stationary distribution of an irreducible, aperiodic chain by power iteration
func stationaryDistribution(P [][]float64, iterations int) []float64 {
    k := len(P)
    v := make([]float64, k)
    for i := range v {
        v[i] = 1.0 / float64(k) // start from the uniform distribution
    }
    for it := 0; it < iterations; it++ {
        next := make([]float64, k)
        for i := 0; i < k; i++ {
            for j := 0; j < k; j++ {
                next[j] += v[i] * P[i][j] // one step: v * P
            }
        }
        v = next
    }
    return v
}
### grundy
--idea
Many games are sums of independent sub-games: you pick one sub-game each turn and move there. The Sprague-Grundy theorem says every such game position (impartial: both players have the same moves, and the player who cannot move loses) behaves exactly like a Nim pile of some size, called its Grundy number. The sum of games then behaves like Nim with those pile sizes: the player to move wins exactly when the XOR of the Grundy numbers is not zero.
How to get the Grundy number of a position: it is the mex ("minimum excluded value") of the Grundy numbers of the positions you can move to, that is, the smallest non-negative integer not in that set. A position with no moves has Grundy number 0 (the mex of the empty set). Example: a pile where you may remove 1 or 2 stones. `g(0) = 0`. `g(1) = mex{g(0)} = mex{0} = 1`. `g(2) = mex{g(1), g(0)} = mex{1, 0} = 2`. `g(3) = mex{g(2), g(1)} = mex{2, 1} = 0`. Then it repeats `0, 1, 2, 0, 1, 2, ...`. Two such piles of sizes 4 and 5: `g = 1` and `2`, XOR `3`, not zero, so the first player wins.
--build
1. Define a position and its moves. For a pile of `n` stones with allowed removals `moves`, the positions reachable from `i` are `i - m` for each `m` in `moves` with `m <= i`.
2. Compute `g[i]` for `i = 0, 1, ..., n` in increasing order, so every option is ready. `g[0] = 0`.
3. For each `i`, collect the Grundy numbers of the options in a bitmask: `mask = setBit(mask, g[i - m])` (the `setBit` piece). Grundy numbers are at most the number of moves, so a machine word is enough here.
4. The mex is the first bit that is not set: start at 0 and increase while `getBit(mask, v)` is true. Set `g[i]` to that.
5. For a sum of games (several piles, or any independent parts), compute the Grundy number of each part and XOR them together (`gameValue`). The sum is a first-player win if and only if this XOR is non-zero.
6. To play: to make the XOR zero, pick the part whose Grundy number `x` can be reduced to `x XOR total` (some move leads to a position with exactly that Grundy number, because the mex property guarantees every smaller value is reachable).
7. Why it works: a position with Grundy number `g` can reach positions with every smaller Grundy number, and none with `g` itself. So it acts like a Nim heap of size `g`: you can lower it to any smaller value, and any move that "raises" it can be undone by the opponent moving back to `g`.
--think
- Signal: a game played on several independent boards, piles, or components where a move touches one component; "who wins" with optimal play; a game whose single-component version is not plain Nim.
- Decide the components and the moves of one component, compute its Grundy numbers (often periodic, so look for the pattern), then XOR across components.
- Neighbours: Nim (Grundy number equals the pile size), game DP (a single component, win or lose only), misere games (different rule at the end), Wythoff's game (not a sum of independent piles, since a move changes both), XOR basis and bit tricks (the XOR step).
- Brute force first: compute `g` for small sizes and print the table; look for periodicity or a formula.
- Pitfalls: using win/lose instead of numbers for a component (win/lose cannot be combined across components); mex of the wrong set (include only reachable positions); components that are not independent; misere play (the theorem fails at the end).
--cost
Table of size `n` with `k` moves: O(n * k) time, O(n) space.
--uses
u-bit
--java
// Grundy numbers g[0..n] of a pile where one move removes some m in moves[] stones
static int[] grundyTable(int n, int[] moves) {
    int[] g = new int[n + 1];                         // g[0] = 0: no moves
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        int mask = 0;                                 // set of Grundy numbers of the options
        for (int m : moves)
            if (i >= m) mask = setBit(mask, g[i - m]);
        int v = 0;
        while (getBit(mask, v)) v++;                  // mex: the first value not present
        g[i] = v;
    }
    return g;
}

// value of a sum of piles: XOR of the Grundy numbers; non-zero means the first player wins
static int gameValue(int[] piles, int[] g) {
    int x = 0;
    for (int p : piles) x ^= g[p];
    return x;
}
--py
def grundy_table(n, moves):
    """Grundy numbers g[0..n] of a pile where one move removes some m in moves stones."""
    g = [0] * (n + 1)                                 # g[0] = 0: no moves
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        mask = 0                                      # set of Grundy numbers of the options
        for m in moves:
            if i >= m:
                mask = set_bit(mask, g[i - m])
        v = 0
        while get_bit(mask, v):
            v += 1                                    # mex: the first value not present
        g[i] = v
    return g

def game_value(piles, g):
    """XOR of the Grundy numbers; non-zero means the first player wins."""
    x = 0
    for p in piles:
        x ^= g[p]
    return x
--cpp
// Grundy numbers g[0..n] of a pile where one move removes some m in moves[] stones
vector<int> grundyTable(int n, const vector<int>& moves) {
    vector<int> g(n + 1, 0);                          // g[0] = 0: no moves
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        int mask = 0;                                 // set of Grundy numbers of the options
        for (int m : moves)
            if (i >= m) mask = setBit(mask, g[i - m]);
        int v = 0;
        while (getBit(mask, v)) v++;                  // mex: the first value not present
        g[i] = v;
    }
    return g;
}

// value of a sum of piles: XOR of the Grundy numbers; non-zero means the first player wins
int gameValue(const vector<int>& piles, const vector<int>& g) {
    int x = 0;
    for (int p : piles) x ^= g[p];
    return x;
}
--go
// Grundy numbers g[0..n] of a pile where one move removes some m in moves stones
func grundyTable(n int, moves []int) []int {
    g := make([]int, n+1) // g[0] = 0: no moves
    for i := 1; i <= n; i++ {
        mask := 0 // set of Grundy numbers of the options
        for _, m := range moves {
            if i >= m {
                mask = setBit(mask, g[i-m])
            }
        }
        v := 0
        for getBit(mask, v) { // mex: the first value not present
            v++
        }
        g[i] = v
    }
    return g
}

// value of a sum of piles: XOR of the Grundy numbers; non-zero means the first player wins
func gameValue(piles, g []int) int {
    x := 0
    for _, p := range piles {
        x ^= g[p]
    }
    return x
}
### misere
--idea
In misere play the rule is flipped: the player who takes the last stone loses. For most games this changes everything, but for Nim the answer is almost the same as normal play. Rule: if every pile has at most one stone, count the piles of size 1: the player to move loses when that count is odd (they are forced to take the last stone in the end), and wins when it is even. Otherwise (some pile has two or more stones), play exactly as in normal Nim: the player to move loses exactly when the XOR of the pile sizes is 0.
Why the exception works: as long as a big pile exists, the winner of normal Nim can steer the game so that, when the big piles run out, the position is right for them. Example: piles `1, 1, 1`. All piles are at most 1 and the count of ones is 3, odd, so the mover loses (each takes one in turn, and the mover takes the last). Piles `2, 1`: some pile is at least 2, XOR is `3`, non-zero, so the mover wins: take the whole pile of 2 leaving a single 1, and the opponent must take the last stone. Piles `1, 1`: count of ones is 2, even, mover wins: take one, the opponent takes the last.
--build
1. Compute `big = (some pile has size >= 2)`.
2. If `big` is false, count the piles of size exactly 1: the position is losing for the mover when that count is odd.
3. If `big` is true, compute the XOR of all piles. The position is losing for the mover exactly when the XOR is 0 (same as normal Nim).
4. To find a winning move, try every move (choose a pile `i` and a smaller size `s`) and return the first one that leaves a losing position for the opponent by the rule above. This is slower than a formula but always correct and small.
5. Proof sketch: from a position with a big pile and non-zero XOR, the normal-Nim move to XOR 0 leaves a position that still has a big pile, or if all piles are now at most one, the move can be changed (reduce to 0 or 1) to leave an odd number of ones. From XOR 0 with a big pile, every move gives non-zero XOR (or a small position with an even count of ones).
--think
- Signal: "the player who takes the last item loses", "whoever makes the last move loses", Nim with a reversed ending.
- Decide the structure: if it is plain Nim, use the rule above. For other games, misere analysis is hard: the Sprague-Grundy numbers no longer simply XOR. Misere quotient theory exists, but contest problems usually reduce to small cases checked by brute force.
- Neighbours: Nim (normal play), Sprague-Grundy (normal play only), game DP (always works with small states, just flip the base case), Wythoff.
- Always brute-force the small cases to validate the rule: win or lose DP over all pile configurations up to size 3 or 4.
- Pitfalls: applying the normal-play rule when all piles are at most 1; treating zero ones as odd; assuming misere Grundy numbers behave like normal ones; for a position with no stones at all, the player who must move has already won (the opponent took the last stone).
--cost
Evaluation O(n) for `n` piles. Searching all moves O(n * largest pile * n).
--java
// true if the player to move loses in misere Nim (the one who takes the last stone loses)
static boolean misereIsLosing(int[] piles) {
    boolean big = false;
    int ones = 0, x = 0;
    for (int p : piles) {
        if (p >= 2) big = true;
        if (p == 1) ones++;
        x ^= p;
    }
    if (!big) return ones % 2 == 1;               // only 0s and 1s: an odd count of ones loses
    return x == 0;                                // otherwise normal Nim
}

// a winning move {pile index, new size}, or null when every move loses
static int[] misereMove(int[] piles) {
    for (int i = 0; i < piles.length; i++)
        for (int s = 0; s < piles[i]; s++) {
            int old = piles[i];
            piles[i] = s;
            boolean ok = misereIsLosing(piles);   // does the opponent now lose?
            piles[i] = old;
            if (ok) return new int[]{i, s};
        }
    return null;
}
--py
def misere_is_losing(piles):
    """True if the player to move loses in misere Nim (the one who takes the last stone loses)."""
    big = any(p >= 2 for p in piles)
    if not big:
        return sum(1 for p in piles if p == 1) % 2 == 1   # only 0s and 1s: an odd count of ones loses
    x = 0
    for p in piles:
        x ^= p
    return x == 0                                          # otherwise normal Nim

def misere_move(piles):
    """A winning move (pile index, new size), or None when every move loses."""
    piles = list(piles)
    for i in range(len(piles)):
        for s in range(piles[i]):
            old = piles[i]
            piles[i] = s
            ok = misere_is_losing(piles)                   # does the opponent now lose?
            piles[i] = old
            if ok:
                return i, s
    return None
--cpp
// true if the player to move loses in misere Nim (the one who takes the last stone loses)
bool misereIsLosing(const vector<int>& piles) {
    bool big = false;
    int ones = 0, x = 0;
    for (int p : piles) {
        if (p >= 2) big = true;
        if (p == 1) ones++;
        x ^= p;
    }
    if (!big) return ones % 2 == 1;               // only 0s and 1s: an odd count of ones loses
    return x == 0;                                // otherwise normal Nim
}

// a winning move {pile index, new size}, or {-1, -1} when every move loses
pair<int, int> misereMove(vector<int> piles) {
    for (size_t i = 0; i < piles.size(); i++)
        for (int s = 0; s < piles[i]; s++) {
            int old = piles[i];
            piles[i] = s;
            bool ok = misereIsLosing(piles);      // does the opponent now lose?
            piles[i] = old;
            if (ok) return {(int)i, s};
        }
    return {-1, -1};
}
--go
// true if the player to move loses in misere Nim (the one who takes the last stone loses)
func misereIsLosing(piles []int) bool {
    big := false
    ones, x := 0, 0
    for _, p := range piles {
        if p >= 2 {
            big = true
        }
        if p == 1 {
            ones++
        }
        x ^= p
    }
    if !big { // only 0s and 1s: an odd count of ones loses
        return ones%2 == 1
    }
    return x == 0 // otherwise normal Nim
}

// a winning move (pile index, new size); ok is false when every move loses
func misereMove(piles []int) (pile, size int, ok bool) {
    for i := range piles {
        for s := 0; s < piles[i]; s++ {
            old := piles[i]
            piles[i] = s
            lose := misereIsLosing(piles) // does the opponent now lose?
            piles[i] = old
            if lose {
                return i, s, true
            }
        }
    }
    return 0, 0, false
}
### wythoff
--idea
Wythoff's game has two piles of stones. A move either removes any positive number of stones from one pile, or removes the same positive number from both piles. The player who takes the last stone wins. The extra "both piles" move breaks the XOR rule of Nim, but the losing positions (those where the player to move loses) have a famous form tied to the golden ratio `phi = (1 + sqrt 5) / 2 = 1.618...`.
The losing positions are the pairs `(a_k, b_k)` with `a_k = floor(k * phi)` and `b_k = a_k + k`: `(0, 0)`, `(1, 2)`, `(3, 5)`, `(4, 7)`, `(6, 10)`, `(8, 13)`, and so on. They can also be built greedily: `a_k` is the smallest number not used yet in any earlier pair, and `b_k = a_k + k`. Every number appears exactly once across all pairs. Example: from `(3, 4)` the player to move wins by removing 2 from both piles, reaching `(1, 2)`, a losing position. Why the pairs work: from `(a_k, b_k)`, any move changes one pile (landing on a number used elsewhere) or both (changing the difference `b - a` while staying on a number that already belongs to another pair), so you can never move into another losing pair; and from any other position some move reaches one.
--build
1. Normalise the position to `x <= y` and let `k = y - x` (the difference).
2. The position is losing exactly when `x = a_k = floor(k * phi)`. Check this without floating point: `floor(k * phi) = floor((k + floor(k * sqrt 5)) / 2)`, and `floor(k * sqrt 5) = isqrt(5 * k * k)`, the integer square root.
3. Implement `isqrt` exactly (start from the floating point root and correct by one or two).
4. Greedy table as a cross-check: keep a list `used` of booleans. For `k = 0, 1, 2, ...`: take `a = ` the smallest unused number, set `b = a + k`, mark both used, and record the pair. This is `wythoffPairs`.
5. To find a winning move from a non-losing position: try the moves (take `t` from the first pile, from the second pile, or `t` from both) and return the first that gives a losing position. Moves are at most `x + y`, so this is fast for moderate sizes.
6. Why the formula is right: the pairs `(a_k, b_k)` partition the positive integers (Beatty's theorem for the two irrational numbers `phi` and `phi^2 = phi + 1`, whose reciprocals add to 1), which is exactly the "smallest unused number" rule.
--think
- Signal: two piles with a diagonal move, "remove from one or take equally from both", a game on a grid where a queen moves toward a corner (queen moves down, left, or diagonally down-left is the same as Wythoff's game).
- Decide whether the game is plain Nim (single-pile moves only, use XOR) or has the diagonal move (use these pairs). Three or more piles with such a move have no neat rule.
- Neighbours: Nim and Sprague-Grundy numbers (the Grundy numbers of Wythoff positions are irregular, which is why the losing-set structure is the right tool), game DP (brute force for checking), Fibonacci and golden ratio (Zeckendorf-style variants), continued fractions (the irrational `phi` with its `1,1,1,...` expansion).
- Always check small cases by DP: build a win or lose table up to 20 by 20 and compare with the formula.
- Pitfalls: floating point error in `k * phi` for large `k` (use the integer square root version); forgetting to sort the piles; thinking `(0, 0)` is not losing (it is, the player to move has no move); off-by-one in `k`.
--cost
Check in O(1) (an integer square root). Greedy table up to `n` pairs in O(n).
--java
static long isqrt(long n) {
    long r = (long) Math.sqrt((double) n);
    while (r * r > n) r--;                            // correct the floating point estimate
    while ((r + 1) * (r + 1) <= n) r++;
    return r;
}

// true if the player to move loses from piles (x, y)
static boolean wythoffIsLosing(long x, long y) {
    if (x > y) { long t = x; x = y; y = t; }
    long k = y - x;
    long a = (k + isqrt(5 * k * k)) / 2;              // floor(k * golden ratio), exactly
    return x == a;
}

// the first `count` losing pairs, built greedily: a = smallest unused number, b = a + k
static long[][] wythoffPairs(int count) {
    boolean[] used = new boolean[4 * count + 10];
    long[][] pairs = new long[count][2];
    int a = 0;
    for (int k = 0; k < count; k++) {
        while (used[a]) a++;
        pairs[k][0] = a;
        pairs[k][1] = a + k;
        used[a] = true;
        used[a + k] = true;
    }
    return pairs;
}

// a winning move {new x, new y}, or null when the position is already lost
static long[] wythoffMove(long x, long y) {
    if (wythoffIsLosing(x, y)) return null;
    for (long t = 1; t <= x; t++) if (wythoffIsLosing(x - t, y)) return new long[]{x - t, y};
    for (long t = 1; t <= y; t++) if (wythoffIsLosing(x, y - t)) return new long[]{x, y - t};
    for (long t = 1; t <= Math.min(x, y); t++) if (wythoffIsLosing(x - t, y - t)) return new long[]{x - t, y - t};
    return null;
}
--py
from math import isqrt

def wythoff_is_losing(x, y):
    """True if the player to move loses from piles (x, y)."""
    if x > y:
        x, y = y, x
    k = y - x
    a = (k + isqrt(5 * k * k)) // 2                   # floor(k * golden ratio), exactly
    return x == a

def wythoff_pairs(count):
    """The first `count` losing pairs, built greedily: a = smallest unused number, b = a + k."""
    used = set()
    pairs = []
    a = 0
    for k in range(count):
        while a in used:
            a += 1
        pairs.append((a, a + k))
        used.add(a)
        used.add(a + k)
    return pairs

def wythoff_move(x, y):
    """A winning move (new x, new y), or None when the position is already lost."""
    if wythoff_is_losing(x, y):
        return None
    for t in range(1, x + 1):
        if wythoff_is_losing(x - t, y):
            return x - t, y
    for t in range(1, y + 1):
        if wythoff_is_losing(x, y - t):
            return x, y - t
    for t in range(1, min(x, y) + 1):
        if wythoff_is_losing(x - t, y - t):
            return x - t, y - t
    return None
--cpp
long long isqrtll(long long n) {
    long long r = (long long)sqrtl((long double)n);
    while (r * r > n) r--;                            // correct the floating point estimate
    while ((r + 1) * (r + 1) <= n) r++;
    return r;
}

// true if the player to move loses from piles (x, y)
bool wythoffIsLosing(long long x, long long y) {
    if (x > y) swap(x, y);
    long long k = y - x;
    long long a = (k + isqrtll(5 * k * k)) / 2;       // floor(k * golden ratio), exactly
    return x == a;
}

// the first `count` losing pairs, built greedily: a = smallest unused number, b = a + k
vector<pair<long long, long long>> wythoffPairs(int count) {
    vector<bool> used(4 * count + 10, false);
    vector<pair<long long, long long>> pairs;
    int a = 0;
    for (int k = 0; k < count; k++) {
        while (used[a]) a++;
        pairs.push_back({a, a + k});
        used[a] = true;
        used[a + k] = true;
    }
    return pairs;
}

// a winning move {new x, new y}, or {-1, -1} when the position is already lost
pair<long long, long long> wythoffMove(long long x, long long y) {
    if (wythoffIsLosing(x, y)) return {-1, -1};
    for (long long t = 1; t <= x; t++) if (wythoffIsLosing(x - t, y)) return {x - t, y};
    for (long long t = 1; t <= y; t++) if (wythoffIsLosing(x, y - t)) return {x, y - t};
    for (long long t = 1; t <= min(x, y); t++) if (wythoffIsLosing(x - t, y - t)) return {x - t, y - t};
    return {-1, -1};
}
--go
func isqrt(n int) int {
    r := int(math.Sqrt(float64(n)))
    for r*r > n { // correct the floating point estimate
        r--
    }
    for (r+1)*(r+1) <= n {
        r++
    }
    return r
}

// true if the player to move loses from piles (x, y)
func wythoffIsLosing(x, y int) bool {
    if x > y {
        x, y = y, x
    }
    k := y - x
    a := (k + isqrt(5*k*k)) / 2 // floor(k * golden ratio), exactly
    return x == a
}

// the first count losing pairs, built greedily: a = smallest unused number, b = a + k
func wythoffPairs(count int) [][2]int {
    used := make([]bool, 4*count+10)
    pairs := make([][2]int, count)
    a := 0
    for k := 0; k < count; k++ {
        for used[a] {
            a++
        }
        pairs[k] = [2]int{a, a + k}
        used[a], used[a+k] = true, true
    }
    return pairs
}

// a winning move (new x, new y); ok is false when the position is already lost
func wythoffMove(x, y int) (nx, ny int, ok bool) {
    if wythoffIsLosing(x, y) {
        return 0, 0, false
    }
    for t := 1; t <= x; t++ {
        if wythoffIsLosing(x-t, y) {
            return x - t, y, true
        }
    }
    for t := 1; t <= y; t++ {
        if wythoffIsLosing(x, y-t) {
            return x, y - t, true
        }
    }
    for t := 1; t <= min(x, y); t++ {
        if wythoffIsLosing(x-t, y-t) {
            return x - t, y - t, true
        }
    }
    return 0, 0, false
}
### sternb
--idea
The Stern-Brocot tree is a binary tree that contains every positive fraction exactly once, always in lowest terms.
