It is built with the mediant: the mediant of `a/b` and `c/d` is `(a + c)/(b + d)`, a fraction strictly between them. Start with the two "fractions" `0/1` and `1/0` (think of `1/0` as infinity). Their mediant is `1/1`, the root. Then below `1/1`, the mediant of `0/1` and `1/1` is `1/2` (left child) and of `1/1` and `1/0` is `2/1` (right child). Always take the mediant of the two ends that currently bracket the node.
Every fraction has a unique address: the string of L and R turns from the root. Example: `3/2`: at `1/1`, `3/2 > 1` so go right to `2/1`; `3/2 < 2` so go left to `3/2`. The path is `RL`. Remarkably, the lengths of the runs in the path are the continued fraction terms: for `p/q = [a0; a1, a2, ...]` the path is `R^a0 L^a1 R^a2 ...` with the last run shortened by one. That makes the path computable by the Euclidean algorithm even when it is millions of steps long (like `1/1000000`, which is a million L moves in a row).
--build
1. Set the ends `lo = 0/1` and `hi = 1/0`. The current node is their mediant. At each step compare the target `p/q` with the node `m = (lo.n + hi.n)/(lo.d + hi.d)`: equal means done; smaller means move left (`hi = m`, record `L`); larger means move right (`lo = m`, record `R`). This is `sbFraction` run in reverse: it reads a path and returns the fraction.
2. Fast path by Euclid (`sbPathRuns`): make `p/q` lowest terms. Set `dir = R`. While `q != 0`: take `k = p / q` and record `(dir, k)`; then `(p, q) = (q, p mod q)` and flip `dir` between R and L. The recorded `k` are exactly the continued fraction terms.
3. A zero term (when `p < q`, the first term is 0) is an empty run and is skipped, but the direction still flips. Fix the last run: the final term `a_n` counts one step too many (the fraction itself is reached after `a_n - 1` of those steps), so subtract 1 from it and drop the run if it becomes 0. For `1/1` the whole path is empty.
4. Reconstruct a fraction from a path (`sbFraction`): start with `lo = (0, 1)`, `hi = (1, 0)`; for each `L` or `R`, set `m = lo + hi`; on `L` set `hi = m`, on `R` set `lo = m`; the answer is the final mediant `lo + hi`.
5. Why the mediant is always in lowest terms: every adjacent pair `(a/b, c/d)` in the tree satisfies `c*b - a*d = 1`, and this is kept by taking mediants, so numerator and denominator of any node are coprime.
6. Search use: to find the fraction with the smallest denominator in an interval, walk down the tree until the node lies inside the interval.
--think
- Signal: "enumerate all fractions", "fraction with smallest denominator between two bounds", "best rational approximation", "binary search over fractions" (binary search over the tree instead of over reals), "Farey sequence" problems.
- Decide: if you need exactness (no floating point), this tree is the tool. For a single approximation question, continued fractions give the same result faster.
- Neighbours: continued fractions (the compressed form of the path), gcd and Euclid (the run lengths), Farey sequences (the fractions up to a bound, neighbours satisfy `bc - ad = 1`), mediants, binary search (on the tree depth).
- Remember the left-right meaning: `L` goes to smaller values, `R` to larger.
- Pitfalls: not reducing `p/q` before computing the path; the off-by-one in the last run; expanding a path of length 10^9 explicitly (use runs); mixing numerator and denominator when going to `1/0`.
--cost
Runs by Euclid O(log(max(p, q))). Expanding a path costs its length.
--java
// path to p/q (lowest terms, positive) as runs {direction ('R' or 'L'), length}
static List<long[]> sbPathRuns(long p, long q) {
    List<long[]> runs = new ArrayList<>();
    char dir = 'R';
    while (q != 0) {
        long k = p / q;
        if (k > 0) runs.add(new long[]{dir, k});   // a continued fraction term (skip empty runs)
        long t = p % q; p = q; q = t;              // Euclid step
        dir = dir == 'R' ? 'L' : 'R';              // directions alternate
    }
    long[] last = runs.get(runs.size() - 1);
    last[1]--;                                     // the final term overshoots by one
    if (last[1] == 0) runs.remove(runs.size() - 1);
    return runs;
}

// the fraction {numerator, denominator} at the end of a path string such as "RL"
static long[] sbFraction(String path) {
    long ln = 0, ld = 1, hn = 1, hd = 0;           // lo = 0/1, hi = 1/0
    for (char c : path.toCharArray()) {
        long mn = ln + hn, md = ld + hd;           // mediant
        if (c == 'L') { hn = mn; hd = md; } else { ln = mn; ld = md; }
    }
    return new long[]{ln + hn, ld + hd};
}
--py
def sb_path_runs(p, q):
    """Path to p/q (lowest terms, positive) as runs [direction ('R' or 'L'), length]."""
    runs = []
    d = "R"
    while q != 0:
        if p // q > 0:
            runs.append([d, p // q])               # a continued fraction term (skip empty runs)
        p, q = q, p % q                            # Euclid step
        d = "L" if d == "R" else "R"               # directions alternate
    runs[-1][1] -= 1                               # the final term overshoots by one
    if runs[-1][1] == 0:
        runs.pop()
    return runs

def sb_fraction(path):
    """The fraction (numerator, denominator) at the end of a path string such as "RL"."""
    ln, ld, hn, hd = 0, 1, 1, 0                    # lo = 0/1, hi = 1/0
    for c in path:
        mn, md = ln + hn, ld + hd                  # mediant
        if c == "L":
            hn, hd = mn, md
        else:
            ln, ld = mn, md
    return ln + hn, ld + hd
--cpp
// path to p/q (lowest terms, positive) as runs {direction ('R' or 'L'), length}
vector<pair<char, long long>> sbPathRuns(long long p, long long q) {
    vector<pair<char, long long>> runs;
    char dir = 'R';
    while (q != 0) {
        if (p / q > 0) runs.push_back({dir, p / q});   // a continued fraction term (skip empty runs)
        long long t = p % q; p = q; q = t;         // Euclid step
        dir = dir == 'R' ? 'L' : 'R';              // directions alternate
    }
    runs.back().second--;                          // the final term overshoots by one
    if (runs.back().second == 0) runs.pop_back();
    return runs;
}

// the fraction {numerator, denominator} at the end of a path string such as "RL"
pair<long long, long long> sbFraction(const string& path) {
    long long ln = 0, ld = 1, hn = 1, hd = 0;      // lo = 0/1, hi = 1/0
    for (char c : path) {
        long long mn = ln + hn, md = ld + hd;      // mediant
        if (c == 'L') { hn = mn; hd = md; } else { ln = mn; ld = md; }
    }
    return {ln + hn, ld + hd};
}
--go
// path to p/q (lowest terms, positive) as runs of {direction ('R' or 'L'), length}
type run struct {
    dir byte
    n   int
}

func sbPathRuns(p, q int) []run {
    var runs []run
    dir := byte('R')
    for q != 0 {
        if k := p / q; k > 0 {
            runs = append(runs, run{dir, k}) // a continued fraction term (skip empty runs)
        }
        p, q = q, p%q // Euclid step
        if dir == 'R' { // directions alternate
            dir = 'L'
        } else {
            dir = 'R'
        }
    }
    last := len(runs) - 1
    runs[last].n-- // the final term overshoots by one
    if runs[last].n == 0 {
        runs = runs[:last]
    }
    return runs
}

// the fraction (numerator, denominator) at the end of a path string such as "RL"
func sbFraction(path string) (int, int) {
    ln, ld, hn, hd := 0, 1, 1, 0 // lo = 0/1, hi = 1/0
    for _, c := range path {
        mn, md := ln+hn, ld+hd // mediant
        if c == 'L' {
            hn, hd = mn, md
        } else {
            ln, ld = mn, md
        }
    }
    return ln + hn, ld + hd
}
### contfrac
--idea
A continued fraction writes a number as `a0 + 1 / (a1 + 1 / (a2 + 1 / (a3 + ...)))`, written `[a0; a1, a2, a3, ...]`. For a fraction `p/q` it is computed by the Euclidean algorithm: take the integer part, flip the remainder, repeat. Example: `415/93`: `415 = 4*93 + 43` so `a0 = 4`; then `93 = 2*43 + 7` so `a1 = 2`; `43 = 6*7 + 1` so `a2 = 6`; `7 = 7*1 + 0` so `a3 = 7`. So `415/93 = [4; 2, 6, 7]`.
The value of cutting the expansion short is that the truncations, called convergents, are the best rational approximations of the number: no fraction with a smaller denominator is closer. They follow a recurrence: `h(k) = a(k) * h(k-1) + h(k-2)` and `den(k) = a(k) * den(k-1) + den(k-2)`, starting from `h(-1) = 1, den(-1) = 0` and `h(-2) = 0, den(-2) = 1`. For `[4; 2, 6, 7]`: `4/1`, `9/2` (since `2*4 + 1 = 9`, `2*1 + 0 = 2`), `58/13` (`6*9 + 4`, `6*2 + 1`), `415/93` (`7*58 + 9`, `7*13 + 2`). Between convergents lie semiconvergents, which matter when the denominator is capped.
--build
1. Expansion (`contFrac`): while `q != 0`: append `p / q` to the list, then replace `(p, q)` by `(q, p mod q)`. This is the Euclidean algorithm and it ends in `O(log)` steps.
2. Convergents (`convergents`): keep `(h1, k1) = (1, 0)` and `(h2, k2) = (0, 1)` (the two previous ones). For each term `a`: `h = a * h1 + h2`, `k = a * k1 + k2`; record `(h, k)`; shift `(h2, k2) = (h1, k1)` and `(h1, k1) = (h, k)`.
3. Best approximation with denominator at most `N` (`bestApprox`): run the recurrence until the next denominator would exceed `N`. Let `(h1, k1)` be the last convergent that fits and `(h2, k2)` the one before it, with `a` the next term. The largest allowed multiple is `t = (N - k2) / k1` (integer division). The semiconvergent is `(h2 + t * h1) / (k2 + t * k1)`.
4. The best fraction is whichever of the last convergent and this semiconvergent is closer to `p/q`. Compare exactly using cross products: the distance of `a/b` from `p/q` is `|p*b - a*q| / (q*b)`, so compare `|p*b1 - a1*q| * b2` against `|p*b2 - a2*q| * b1` without division. (If the expansion ends before the denominator exceeds `N`, `p/q` itself is the answer.)
5. Why the best approximations are convergents: two consecutive convergents satisfy `h(k)*den(k-1) - h(k-1)*den(k) = (-1)^(k-1)`, so they bracket the number tightly, and no fraction with a smaller denominator can fit inside that gap.
6. For real numbers or square roots the same recurrence works with the terms produced by repeatedly taking the integer part and inverting the fractional part.
--think
- Signal: "best rational approximation with denominator up to N", "approximate this ratio by a small fraction", gear ratio and calendar problems, "closest fraction", Pell equations, periodic structure of a quadratic irrational.
- Decide: if the target is a fraction use the Euclidean version; for irrational numbers (such as the square root of `d`) generate terms by the standard integer recurrence; floating point only works for a few terms.
- Neighbours: Euclid and gcd (the same computation), Stern-Brocot tree (the path is the list of terms), Pell equations (use the convergents of the square root), floor sums (the same Euclid-like structure), Farey sequences.
- Bounds on error: the convergent `h/k` satisfies `|x - h/k| < 1 / k^2`, which is why they are the "best" in a strong sense.
- Pitfalls: forgetting the semiconvergent when a denominator cap is given (the last convergent is not always the best); overflow in the cross-product comparison (use `__int128` or 64-bit with small inputs); starting values of the recurrence; negative numbers (floor division).
--cost
Time O(log(max(p, q))). Space O(number of terms).
--java
// continued fraction terms [a0; a1, a2, ...] of p/q (p, q > 0)
static List<Long> contFrac(long p, long q) {
    List<Long> a = new ArrayList<>();
    while (q != 0) {
        a.add(p / q);                               // integer part
        long t = p % q; p = q; q = t;               // flip the remainder (Euclid)
    }
    return a;
}

// convergents {h, k} for the terms a
static List<long[]> convergents(List<Long> a) {
    List<long[]> res = new ArrayList<>();
    long h1 = 1, k1 = 0, h2 = 0, k2 = 1;
    for (long t : a) {
        long h = t * h1 + h2, k = t * k1 + k2;      // the convergent recurrence
        res.add(new long[]{h, k});
        h2 = h1; k2 = k1; h1 = h; k1 = k;
    }
    return res;
}

// the fraction a/b closest to p/q with b <= N (p, q small enough that cross products fit in a long)
static long[] bestApprox(long p, long q, long N) {
    long h1 = 1, k1 = 0, h2 = 0, k2 = 1;
    long a = p, b = q;
    while (b != 0) {
        long t = a / b;
        long h = t * h1 + h2, k = t * k1 + k2;
        if (k > N) {                                // the next convergent is too big: try a semiconvergent
            long m = (N - k2) / k1;
            long sh = h2 + m * h1, sk = k2 + m * k1;
            long dOld = Math.abs(p * k1 - h1 * q) * sk, dNew = Math.abs(p * sk - sh * q) * k1;
            return dNew < dOld ? new long[]{sh, sk} : new long[]{h1, k1};
        }
        h2 = h1; k2 = k1; h1 = h; k1 = k;
        long r = a % b; a = b; b = r;
    }
    return new long[]{h1, k1};                      // p/q itself fits
}
--py
def cont_frac(p, q):
    """Continued fraction terms [a0; a1, a2, ...] of p/q (p, q > 0)."""
    a = []
    while q != 0:
        a.append(p // q)                            # integer part
        p, q = q, p % q                             # flip the remainder (Euclid)
    return a

def convergents(a):
    """Convergents (h, k) for the terms a."""
    res = []
    h1, k1, h2, k2 = 1, 0, 0, 1
    for t in a:
        h, k = t * h1 + h2, t * k1 + k2             # the convergent recurrence
        res.append((h, k))
        h2, k2, h1, k1 = h1, k1, h, k
    return res

def best_approx(p, q, N):
    """The fraction a/b closest to p/q with b <= N."""
    h1, k1, h2, k2 = 1, 0, 0, 1
    a, b = p, q
    while b != 0:
        t = a // b
        h, k = t * h1 + h2, t * k1 + k2
        if k > N:                                   # the next convergent is too big: try a semiconvergent
            m = (N - k2) // k1
            sh, sk = h2 + m * h1, k2 + m * k1
            d_old = abs(p * k1 - h1 * q) * sk
            d_new = abs(p * sk - sh * q) * k1
            return (sh, sk) if d_new < d_old else (h1, k1)
        h2, k2, h1, k1 = h1, k1, h, k
        a, b = b, a % b
    return h1, k1                                   # p/q itself fits
--cpp
// continued fraction terms [a0; a1, a2, ...] of p/q (p, q > 0)
vector<long long> contFrac(long long p, long long q) {
    vector<long long> a;
    while (q != 0) {
        a.push_back(p / q);                         // integer part
        long long t = p % q; p = q; q = t;          // flip the remainder (Euclid)
    }
    return a;
}

// convergents {h, k} for the terms a
vector<pair<long long, long long>> convergents(const vector<long long>& a) {
    vector<pair<long long, long long>> res;
    long long h1 = 1, k1 = 0, h2 = 0, k2 = 1;
    for (long long t : a) {
        long long h = t * h1 + h2, k = t * k1 + k2; // the convergent recurrence
        res.push_back({h, k});
        h2 = h1; k2 = k1; h1 = h; k1 = k;
    }
    return res;
}

// the fraction a/b closest to p/q with b <= N (__int128 keeps the cross products safe)
pair<long long, long long> bestApprox(long long p, long long q, long long N) {
    long long h1 = 1, k1 = 0, h2 = 0, k2 = 1;
    long long a = p, b = q;
    while (b != 0) {
        long long t = a / b;
        long long h = t * h1 + h2, k = t * k1 + k2;
        if (k > N) {                                // the next convergent is too big: try a semiconvergent
            long long m = (N - k2) / k1;
            long long sh = h2 + m * h1, sk = k2 + m * k1;
            __int128 dOld = (__int128)llabs(p * k1 - h1 * q) * sk, dNew = (__int128)llabs(p * sk - sh * q) * k1;
            return dNew < dOld ? make_pair(sh, sk) : make_pair(h1, k1);
        }
        h2 = h1; k2 = k1; h1 = h; k1 = k;
        long long r = a % b; a = b; b = r;
    }
    return {h1, k1};                                // p/q itself fits
}

// continued fraction terms [a0; a1, a2, ...] of p/q (p, q > 0)
func contFrac(p, q int) []int {
    var a []int
    for q != 0 {
        a = append(a, p/q)  // integer part
        p, q = q, p%q       // flip the remainder (Euclid)
    }
    return a
}

// convergents (h, k) for the terms a
func convergents(a []int) [][2]int {
    var res [][2]int
    h1, k1, h2, k2 := 1, 0, 0, 1
    for _, t := range a {
        h, k := t*h1+h2, t*k1+k2 // the convergent recurrence
        res = append(res, [2]int{h, k})
        h2, k2, h1, k1 = h1, k1, h, k
    }
    return res
}

func abs(x int) int {
    if x < 0 {
        return -x
    }
    return x
}

// the fraction a/b closest to p/q with b <= N (p, q small enough that cross products fit in an int)
func bestApprox(p, q, N int) (int, int) {
    h1, k1, h2, k2 := 1, 0, 0, 1
    a, b := p, q
    for b != 0 {
        t := a / b
        h, k := t*h1+h2, t*k1+k2
        if k > N { // the next convergent is too big: try a semiconvergent
            m := (N - k2) / k1
            sh, sk := h2+m*h1, k2+m*k1
            dOld := abs(p*k1-h1*q) * sk
            dNew := abs(p*sk-sh*q) * k1
            if dNew < dOld {
                return sh, sk
            }
            return h1, k1
        }
        h2, k2, h1, k1 = h1, k1, h, k
        a, b = b, a%b
    }
    return h1, k1 // p/q itself fits
}
### pell
--idea
A Pell equation is `x^2 - d*y^2 = 1` where `d` is a positive whole number that is not a perfect square, and we want whole-number solutions `(x, y)` with `y > 0`. It always has infinitely many, and they all grow from one smallest ("fundamental") solution. Example: `d = 2`: `3^2 - 2*2^2 = 9 - 8 = 1`, so `(3, 2)`. For `d = 7`: `(8, 3)` since `64 - 63 = 1`. The smallest solutions can be enormous: for `d = 61` it is `x = 1766319049`, `y = 226153980`.
The link to continued fractions: if `x^2 - d y^2 = 1` then `x/y` is extremely close to `sqrt(d)`, so `x/y` must be one of the convergents of `sqrt(d)`. The continued fraction of a square root is periodic: `sqrt(d) = [a0; a1, a2, ..., ar]` with the pattern repeating, and `a_r = 2 * a0` ends each period. The fundamental solution is the convergent at the end of the first period if the period length `r` is even, and at the end of the second period if `r` is odd. For `d = 7`: `sqrt 7 = [2; 1, 1, 1, 4]`, `r = 4` (even), the convergents are `2/1, 3/1, 5/2, 8/3`: the third (index 3) gives `(8, 3)`.
--build
1. Let `a0 = floor(sqrt(d))` (the `sqrtFloor` piece). If `a0 * a0 = d`, the number is a perfect square and there is no solution: return nothing.
2. Generate the terms of `sqrt(d)` with an integer recurrence (no floating point): start `m = 0`, `dd = 1`, `a = a0`. Repeat: `m = dd * a - m`; `dd = (d - m * m) / dd`; `a = (a0 + m) / dd`. These `a` values are `a1, a2, ...`.
3. Stop when `a = 2 * a0`: that term is the last of the period. Let `r` be the number of terms collected (the period length) and keep them in a list `per[0..r-1]`.
4. Decide which convergent to take: index `r - 1` if `r` is even, else `2r - 1` (going around the period twice).
5. Compute convergents with the standard recurrence: `h(-1) = 1, k(-1) = 0`, `h(0) = a0, k(0) = 1`, and for `i >= 1` use `a = per[(i - 1) mod r]`, `h(i) = a * h(i-1) + h(i-2)`, `k(i) = a * k(i-1) + k(i-2)`.
6. The convergent `(h, k)` at the chosen index is the fundamental solution `(x, y)`. Verify: `x^2 - d*y^2 = 1`.
7. Further solutions: from `(x1, y1)`, the next ones come from `(x_(n+1), y_(n+1)) = (x1 * x_n + d * y1 * y_n, x1 * y_n + y1 * x_n)`, because `(x + y*sqrt(d))` multiplies like a number: `(x1 + y1 sqrt d)^n`.
8. Overflow: `x` can be astronomically large (hundreds of digits for `d` in the thousands). Use `long` only for small `d` (below about 100); use `BigInteger` in Java and big integers (or `__int128` for medium `d`) elsewhere. Python is exact.
--think
- Signal: "find integers `x, y` with `x^2 - d y^2 = 1`", "the n-th number that is both triangular and square" (reduces to Pell), "perfect square that is also a sum of consecutive integers", Diophantine equations with a quadratic form.
- Decide: if the equation is `x^2 - d y^2 = -1` or has another right side `N`, related continued fraction methods exist (the intermediate convergents give the `-1` case when the period is odd); only `= 1` is covered here.
- Neighbours: continued fractions (the engine), integer square root (the start), Stern-Brocot tree (the best-approximation picture), modular square roots and quadratic residues (to know whether similar equations are solvable), big integers (the size of solutions).
- Always verify the result by plugging in; and check the period length parity before choosing the index.
- Pitfalls: floating-point square roots (use the integer recurrence); mixing up the index (`r - 1` versus `2r - 1`); not detecting perfect squares; overflow, and the time to print huge answers; the stop condition is `a = 2 * a0`, not `dd = 1`.
--cost
Time O(r) terms, where the period `r` is at most about `2 * sqrt(d) * log d`; each convergent step is O(1) with machine integers (more with big integers).
--uses
u-sqrt
--java
// fundamental solution {x, y} of x^2 - d y^2 = 1, or null if d is a perfect square.
// Fits in a long only for small d (below about 100); use BigInteger for larger d.
static long[] pell(long d) {
    long a0 = sqrtFloor(d);                           // the sqrtFloor piece
    if (a0 * a0 == d) return null;                    // perfect square: no solution
    List<Long> per = new ArrayList<>();
    long m = 0, dd = 1, a = a0;
    do {
        m = dd * a - m;
        dd = (d - m * m) / dd;
        a = (a0 + m) / dd;                            // next term of the continued fraction
        per.add(a);
    } while (a != 2 * a0);                            // the last term of the period
    int r = per.size();
    int target = (r % 2 == 0) ? r - 1 : 2 * r - 1;    // index of the convergent we want
    long hPrev = 1, kPrev = 0, h = a0, k = 1;         // convergents at index -1 and 0
    for (int i = 1; i <= target; i++) {
        long t = per.get((i - 1) % r);
        long hn = t * h + hPrev, kn = t * k + kPrev;
        hPrev = h; kPrev = k; h = hn; k = kn;
    }
    return new long[]{h, k};
}
--py
def pell(d):
    """Fundamental solution (x, y) of x^2 - d y^2 = 1, or None if d is a perfect square."""
    a0 = sqrt_floor(d)                                # the sqrt_floor piece
    if a0 * a0 == d:
        return None                                   # perfect square: no solution
    per = []
    m, dd, a = 0, 1, a0
    while True:
        m = dd * a - m
        dd = (d - m * m) // dd
        a = (a0 + m) // dd                            # next term of the continued fraction
        per.append(a)
        if a == 2 * a0:
            break                                     # the last term of the period
    r = len(per)
    target = r - 1 if r % 2 == 0 else 2 * r - 1       # index of the convergent we want
    h_prev, k_prev, h, k = 1, 0, a0, 1                # convergents at index -1 and 0
    for i in range(1, target + 1):
        t = per[(i - 1) % r]
        h_prev, k_prev, h, k = h, k, t * h + h_prev, t * k + k_prev
    return h, k
--cpp
// fundamental solution {x, y} of x^2 - d y^2 = 1, or {0, 0} if d is a perfect square.
// Fits in a long long only for small d (below about 100); use big integers for larger d.
pair<long long, long long> pell(long long d) {
    long long a0 = sqrtFloor(d);                      // the sqrtFloor piece
    if (a0 * a0 == d) return {0, 0};                  // perfect square: no solution
    vector<long long> per;
    long long m = 0, dd = 1, a = a0;
    do {
        m = dd * a - m;
        dd = (d - m * m) / dd;
        a = (a0 + m) / dd;                            // next term of the continued fraction
        per.push_back(a);
    } while (a != 2 * a0);                            // the last term of the period
    int r = per.size();
    int target = (r % 2 == 0) ? r - 1 : 2 * r - 1;    // index of the convergent we want
    long long hPrev = 1, kPrev = 0, h = a0, k = 1;    // convergents at index -1 and 0
    for (int i = 1; i <= target; i++) {
        long long t = per[(i - 1) % r];
        long long hn = t * h + hPrev, kn = t * k + kPrev;
        hPrev = h; kPrev = k; h = hn; k = kn;
    }
    return {h, k};
}
--go
// fundamental solution (x, y) of x^2 - d y^2 = 1; ok is false if d is a perfect square.
// Fits in an int only for small d (below about 100); use math/big for larger d.
func pell(d int) (x, y int, ok bool) {
    a0 := sqrtFloor(d) // the sqrtFloor piece
    if a0*a0 == d {    // perfect square: no solution
        return 0, 0, false
    }
    var per []int
    m, dd, a := 0, 1, a0
    for {
        m = dd*a - m
        dd = (d - m*m) / dd
        a = (a0 + m) / dd // next term of the continued fraction
        per = append(per, a)
        if a == 2*a0 { // the last term of the period
            break
        }
    }
    r := len(per)
    target := 2*r - 1 // index of the convergent we want
    if r%2 == 0 {
        target = r - 1
    }
    hPrev, kPrev, h, k := 1, 0, a0, 1 // convergents at index -1 and 0
    for i := 1; i <= target; i++ {
        t := per[(i-1)%r]
        hPrev, kPrev, h, k = h, k, t*h+hPrev, t*k+kPrev
    }
    return h, k, true
}
